**Summary.** The paper analyzes superradiant lasing using a model of atoms driven by spatially correlated pumps, ranging from fully collective to purely local driving. By employing GPU-accelerated TWA simulations, the authors demonstrate that tuning the pump correlation exponent ($\alpha$) provides a powerful knob to suppress recoil heating while maintaining high optical coherence in the emitted light.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses open quantum systems dynamics in cavity QED/optics. The ability to tune coherence and dissipation independently via the pump structure ($\alpha$) is highly relevant for designing robust quantum light sources.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how varying the spatial correlation range ($\alpha$) of an external pump field affects the lasing dynamics in Superradiant (SR) lasers, balancing drive intensity, medium heating, and emitted light coherence.

**Main result.** Correlated pumping allows for a significant reduction in the required driving strength compared to local pumping, while maintaining high optical coherence ($g^{(2)} 	o 1$) even when the pump correlation range is short ($\alpha \approx 1$).

**Method.** The dynamics are simulated using the Truncated Wigner Approximation (TWA), which allows for efficient GPU-accelerated calculation of steady-state observables across a wide parameter space.

**Model / system.** The system models $N$ two-level atoms arranged on a 1D chain, coupled to a single cavity mode. The pumping mechanism is generalized by a power-law decay $w_{ij} \propto (|	ext{i}-	ext{j}|+1)^{-\alpha}$, interpolating between collective ($\alpha=0$) and local ($\alpha	o\infty$) driving.

**Key observables.** Steady-state magnetization ($\langle S_z angle$), intensity, second-order coherence ($g^{(2)}$), and linewidth ($\Delta
u$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The correlation exponent $\alpha$, the pump rate $w$, and system size $N$. Key regimes include $\alpha=0$ (collective) and $\alpha=\infty$ (local).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary approximation used is the Truncated Wigner Approximation (TWA), which maps quantum dynamics onto classical stochastic trajectories.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams showing transitions between different light regimes based on pump rate and $\alpha$, and compare observables like $g^{(2)}$ and linewidth for various pumping limits ($\alpha=0$ vs. finite $\alpha$).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model using a generalized Lindblad master equation, details the TWA methodology suitable for GPU computation, analyzes key observables (coherence, drive strength), and concludes by mapping out how the pump correlation acts as a tunable knob controlling these competing physical effects.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Superradiant (SR) lasers store optical coherence in the atomic medium rather than the cavity field, but the incoherent drive that sustains inversion imposes a trade-off: local pumping yields coherent light at a rate that grows linearly with the atom number $N$, heating the medium through photon recoil, whereas fully collective pumping removes this scaling but limits the emission to partial coherence. We interpolate between these limits employing a spatially correlated pump on a chain of $N$ two-level atoms, with rates decaying with distance between atoms as a power law of exponent $α$. To study systems beyond the reach of exact solutions, we employ the Truncated Wigner Approximation (TWA), whose independent trajectories are ideally suited to GPU parallelism. Harnessing this, we perform a full scan of the steady-state observables for up to $10^4$ atoms at a computational cost that is practical. Our findings indicate that ultra-narrow emission persists for all $α$, while the coherence improves as the pump becomes shorter ranged, with $g^{(2)} \to 1$ surviving at least down to $α\approx 1$, indicating that fully coherent light thus does not require local pumping. The drive strength needed for lasing is reduced by a factor $N^{1-α}$ for $α< 1$, and by $\log N$ as $α\to 1$, parametrically suppressing recoil heating; notably, $α= 1$ matches the far-field envelope of dissipative couplings in free space. The correlation range of the pump thus acts as a knob trading drive intensity, and the heating it causes, against optical coherence.

</details>

Color maps of the real part of the retarded boson propagator, |Re DR(q, ω)|, used to identify the pole structure corresponding to the phase mode. Panels (a) and (b) show the numerical result at E = 0 and E = 0.008, respectively, at dissipation Γ = 0.005, gap parameter ∆= 0.2 and lattice constant a = 0.4. In both panels, well-defined dispersion relations confirm the existence of the Goldstone mode. At E = 0 the linear dispersion relation agrees with the equilibrium benchmark; under the applied field the dispersion remains essentially unchanged, indicating that the Goldstone mode persists in the nonequilibrium steady state while the system remains gapped.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This work proposes that the resistive breakdown of ordered insulators under a DC electric field is governed by the excitation of gapless Goldstone modes associated with broken symmetry. Using Keldysh Green's functions, the authors demonstrate that these collective bosonic excitations heat up much faster than the electrons, predicting a lower critical switching field than single-particle theories suggest.

**Why it may be interesting.** The focus on collective bosonic modes (Goldstone mode) mediating transport breakdown is highly relevant to understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in correlated electron systems, bridging concepts from superfluidity/CDWs into electronic transport physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates an alternative mechanism for resistive breakdown (insulator-to-metal transition) in ordered insulators under a DC electric field, moving beyond single-electron excitation theories.

**Main result.** It shows that fluctuations of the order parameter via the gapless Goldstone mode can heat up much more rapidly than electronic excitations, suggesting collective phase dynamics significantly reduce the critical switching field.

**Method.** The analysis employs the Keldysh Green's function formalism to study non-equilibrium steady states under an applied electric field.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using a Hubbard-like Hamiltonian in 1D with inter-band interactions, leading to spontaneous symmetry breaking and the emergence of both gapped (Higgs) and gapless (Goldstone) bosonic excitations.

**Key observables.** Effective temperatures of the Goldstone mode ($T_b$) versus electronic temperature ($T_e$), and the critical threshold electric fields ($E_{b	ext{th}}$ vs $E_{e	ext{th}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The gap parameter ($\Delta$), hopping integral ($t$), DC electric field strength, and the ratio of $\Delta/\Gamma$ (where $\Gamma$ is a dissipation rate).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses approximations such as the Random-Phase Approximation (RPA) for boson self-energies and initially neglects spatial inhomogeneity.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the free energy landscape, phase textures, electronic band gaps opening at a wavevector Q, and the resulting bosonic dispersion relation, comparing spectral features under different electric fields.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical context (resistive switching) to developing the theoretical framework using Keldysh GF formalism. It first analyzes equilibrium excitations, then extends this to non-equilibrium steady states by incorporating dissipation and the DC field, culminating in a comparison of effective temperatures for electrons and bosons.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Measurements of resistive breakdown in electronic systems under a DC electric field have shown that the threshold fields for the insulator-to-metal transition are significantly lower than predicted by single-electron excitation scenarios, such as the Landau-Zener theory. In this work, we propose an alternate mechanism of destabilizing ordered insulators under a DC electric field by fluctuations of the order parameters through the Goldstone mode excitation. The low-energy bosonic excitations receive energy from accelerated electrons and thus destroy the spontaneous symmetry breaking. Using the Keldysh Gree's function formalism, we numerically confirm that the Goldstone mode remains well-defined in the nonequilibrium steady state, while its nonequilibrium excitations are sensitive to the electric field. The effective temperature of the Goldstone mode increases much more rapidly than the electronic effective temperature, with the bosonic threshold field significantly smaller than the electronic one, which suggests that collective phase dynamics may further reduce the transition field to the experimental range via a purely electronic mechanism.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper analyzes excitation dynamics in dissipative Rydberg lattices by studying Landau-Zener tunneling under time-varying detuning. Using both master equation and non-Hermitian Hamiltonian formalisms, the authors characterize a dynamical crossover regime controlled by the blockade strength. This crossover dictates whether the system exhibits uniform or sublattice-dependent transition probabilities near avoided crossings.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it combines concepts from open quantum systems (dissipation, Lindblad dynamics) with strongly correlated physics (Rydberg interactions, magnetic order) and fundamental non-adiabatic processes (LZ tunneling), providing a detailed map of dynamic phase transitions in controllable quantum simulators.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates excitation dynamics, specifically Landau-Zener tunneling, within a dissipative Rydberg lattice subjected to time-dependent detuning.

**Main result.** The work identifies a dynamical crossover regime where the sublattice population imbalance transitions from vanishing (identical LZ probabilities) to persisting (sublattice-dependent probabilities) as the blockade strength increases.

**Method.** Analysis employs two complementary theoretical frameworks: solving the Lindblad master equation via mean-field approximation and utilizing an effective non-Hermitian Hamiltonian approach for spectral analysis.

**Model / system.** The system is a Rabi-coupled dissipative Rydberg lattice, modeled by a Hamiltonian incorporating detuning, Rabi coupling ($\Omega$), and nearest-neighbor interaction ($V$). Dissipation is included via the Lindblad master equation.

**Key observables.** Landau-Zener transition probabilities (PLZ), sublattice population imbalance, complex energy spectrum eigenvalues, and order parameter $	heta$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Rydberg interaction strength ($V$), Rabi coupling ($\Omega$), dissipation rate ($\gamma$), and the detuning profile $\Delta(t)$. Critical ratios like $\Omega/\gamma$ define spectral regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The non-Hermitian description is an approximation that neglects explicit quantum jump terms, though it proves useful for analyzing avoided crossings. Mean-field approximations are used for interactions.

**Figures summary.** Figures likely show energy spectra loops/branches indicating antiferromagnetic order (as $V$ increases), and phase plots illustrating the crossover between regimes of identical vs. distinct sublattice probabilities based on parameters like $\Omega/\gamma$.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first setting up the full dynamics via the Lindblad master equation, then deriving and analyzing an effective non-Hermitian Hamiltonian approximation. The core analysis focuses on mapping out parameter regimes (governed by $V$ vs. $\Omega/\gamma$) that dictate the nature of the LZ tunneling crossover.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, we investigate the excitation dynamics of a Rabi-coupled dissipative Rydberg lattice with a time-dependent detuning. The system is analyzed using (i) a Lindblad master equation within a mean-field approximation and (ii) an effective non-Hermitian Hamiltonian framework. While the mean-field approach captures the emergence of an antiferromagnetic order in the Rydberg excitation profile, the non-Hermitian description provides direct insight into the complex energy spectrum and its avoided crossings, which govern the Landau$-$Zener dynamics. We identify a regime in which the sublattice population imbalance vanishes near the avoided crossing, resulting in identical Landau$-$Zener probabilities on the two sublattices. Beyond a critical effective blockade strength there is a dynamical crossover to another regime in which the sublattice population imbalance persists through the avoided crossing, giving rise to sublattice-dependent Landau$-$Zener probabilities. Furthermore, Rydberg interactions prolong the lifetime of Landau$-$Zener-induced excitations in the presence of weak dissipation and strong Rabi coupling. In contrast, for weak Rabi coupling, the Rydberg blockade inhibits excitation and suppresses the Landau$-$Zener transition probability.

</details>

By combining theoretical modeling with momentum-resolved cathodoluminescence, they confirm enhanced polariton formation, offering a tunable platform for controlling light-matter interactions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it deals with engineered light-matter interaction in 2D materials, a core topic in quantum optics and open quantum systems research, particularly concerning cavity/waveguide QED effects.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is boosting the coupling strength between excitons (in $	ext{WS}_2$) and guided photonic modes within metal-clad waveguides.

**Main result.** The gold cladding significantly enhances the exciton-photon coupling strength ($G$) to $190 	ext{ meV}$, demonstrating a robust method for controlling light-matter interactions in hybrid heterostructures.

**Method.** The study combines theoretical modeling (solving Maxwell's equations) with experimental characterization using momentum-resolved cathodoluminescence ($	ext{CL}$) spectroscopy.

**Model / system.** The system is an $	ext{Au}/	ext{WS}_2/	ext{Au}$ metal-clad waveguide. The physics is described by analyzing the formation of self-hybridized exciton polaritons, often modeled using a Hopfield Hamiltonian to quantify coupling strength ($G$).

**Figures summary.** Figures show momentum-resolved CL spectra confirming resonances, calculated and measured full dispersion relations for varying thicknesses, and electric field profiles distinguishing mode confinement.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying initial resonances via $	ext{CL}$ spectroscopy to theoretically modeling the coupling using Hamiltonian approaches. It compares results from metal-clad vs. uncladded structures to quantify enhancement and discusses implications for slow polariton propagation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The formation of Fabry Perot and guided wave self hybridized exciton polaritons in two dimensional materials results in long range exciton energy transfer and strong exciton exciton interactions. Here, we demonstrate that the coupling strength between photonic modes and excitons is significantly boosted by embedding the active excitonic layer in a metal clad WS2 waveguide. The photonic modes in this waveguide exhibit modified dispersion properties for both Fabry Perot type and guided wave exciton polaritons compared to pure WS2 flakes, and show an increased couplingr strength. Our results provide a robust approach for controlling exciton photon interactions and their coupling strength in hybrid heterostructures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a rigorous theoretical framework for studying lattice electrons driven by external, nonclassical quantum light fields. It formulates Floquet Green's functions using a shared-history approach that treats the full quantum nature of the driving source nonperturbatively. The results demonstrate how squeezing and phase control can engineer electronic properties beyond what classical laser drives allow.

**Why it may be interesting.** This bridges advanced topics in Floquet engineering (time periodicity) with open quantum systems theory (reservoir/bath dynamics) by incorporating the full quantum nature of the driving field (squeezing), which is highly relevant to modern AMO and quantum optics research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is formulating Floquet Green's functions for noninteracting lattice electrons driven by an externally prescribed, reservoir-stabilized Gaussian quantum light source.

**Main result.** The work provides a theoretical framework for quantum Floquet engineering of condensed matter systems using external quantum light sources, showing finite-coupling quantum corrections beyond classical drives.

**Method.** It employs a shared-history formulation for two-time Green's functions, treating the nonperturbative Peierls coupling and incorporating bath convolutions derived from canonical anticommutation relations.

**Model / system.** The system consists of noninteracting single-band lattice electrons coupled to an external Gaussian quantum light source (reservoir) via the Peierls mechanism. The electron bath provides local linewidths, while the source is modeled as a squeezed coherent steady state.

**Key observables.** Spectral reconstruction and occupancy redistribution are key observables, which can be controlled by the squeezing parameter and phase of the input light.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The squeezing parameter ($r$) and phase ($\phi_0$) of the quantum source provide control knobs beyond classical amplitude modulation. The coupling strength ($\lambda$) is also critical.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include noninteracting single-band electrons, a prescribed (non-backacting) external light source, and the validity of Gaussian approximations for expectation values involving the Peierls exponentials.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 provides an overview diagram illustrating the components: electron–photon system, electron bath, photon source reservoir, and the shared-history construction process involving four active-leg sectors.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the formalism by defining the coupled Hamiltonian, deriving the Green's function structure using a shared-history approach, treating the Peierls coupling nonperturbatively, and concluding with numerical demonstrations on a 1D model showing quantum corrections.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We formulate Floquet Green's functions for noninteracting single-band lattice electrons driven by a reservoir-stabilized single-mode Gaussian quantum light source. The source is prescribed externally and is not updated by the many-electron polarization, while an active electronic probe still conditions the source evolution through the Peierls coupling. The two time arguments of a Green's function share one source history: the lesser and greater components are obtained by convolving a shared-history four-endpoint kernel with the continuous bath kernels before the final source trace, while the bath canonical anticommutation relation yields an equal-time covariance that seeds the retarded and advanced one-leg propagations. The Peierls coupling is treated nonperturbatively within the prescribed-source model, and classical Floquet theory is recovered in the appropriate limit. Numerical calculations on a minimal one-dimensional model show finite-coupling quantum-source corrections beyond a prescribed classical drive, together with spectral reconstruction and occupancy redistribution for squeezed vacuum and squeezed coherent sources. The squeezing parameter and phase provide additional control knobs, beyond classical amplitude modulation, for both sideband structure and occupied weight. This work provides a theoretical framework for quantum Floquet engineering of condensed matter with an externally prescribed quantum light source.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper analyzes the phase transitions in a one-dimensional hybrid contact process by treating both coherent (quantum) and incoherent (classical) interactions. Using mean-field theory and advanced critical analysis methods like CAM, the authors map out the steady-state phase diagram and characterize the nature of the transition points, distinguishing between continuous and discontinuous regimes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses non-equilibrium steady states and quantum phase transitions in open quantum systems, which is a major area of overlap between statistical mechanics and AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the steady-state phase transition behavior of a one-dimensional hybrid contact process (HCP) when both quantum coherent and classical incoherent interactions are present.

**Main result.** The system exhibits distinct transition types: a discontinuous transition via saddle-node bifurcation for varying $\Omega$, and a continuous transition characterized by specific critical exponents ($eta^*$) extracted using the Coherent Anomaly Method.

**Method.** Theoretical analysis employing single-site and cluster mean-field approximations, stability analysis of the Liouvillian spectrum, and advanced techniques like the Coherent Anomaly Method (CAM) to extract true critical points and exponents.

**Model / system.** The model is a one-dimensional HCP on a quantum spin-1/2 chain governed by a Lindblad master equation incorporating coherent ($\Omega$) and incoherent ($\kappa$) rates, alongside local decay ($\Gamma$).

**Key observables.** Steady-state particle density $\langle\hat{n}angle_{ss}$, the order parameter derived from site expectation values, and the coherent anomaly $n_0(L)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relative strengths of interaction rates ($\Omega/\Gamma$ and $\kappa/\Gamma$), system size ($L$), and the critical exponents ($eta^*$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mean-field approximations (single-site MF, CMF) which assume factorization or local correlations; precise critical behavior requires taking thermodynamic limits.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 presents the steady-state phase diagram in the $\Omega-\kappa$ plane showing absorbing, active, and bistable regions. Figure 3 illustrates how varying parameters leads to different transition types (discontinuous vs. continuous).

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines the model via the Lindblad equation and mean-field approach. It then analyzes the phase diagram using MF results, identifying bifurcation points. Finally, it employs CMF and CAM to rigorously characterize the critical exponents associated with the continuous transition.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the steady-state phase transition in a one-dimensional hybrid contact process. We implement the single-site and cluster mean-field approximations based on the effective fields and present all the possible steady states of the system. We show the existence of the stable absorbing and active phases, and the bistable region in the long-time limit. The saddle-node bifurcation is observed at the boundary between the absorbing phase and the bistable region, suggesting a discontinuous phase transition. While the absorbing to active phase transition is continuous. To characterize the nonclassical scaling behavior of the continuous phase transition, we extract the true critical points and exponents by means of the coherent anomaly method.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a general theory to describe how observables evolve in strongly correlated quantum systems coupled to non-Markovian baths. It predicts that the initial time evolution is governed by a universal $t^{2\eta}$ scaling law, which then smoothly transitions into longer-time Markovian behavior. The framework is applicable to ultracold atomic gas simulations.

**Why it may be interesting.** The universal prediction of the $t^{2\eta}$ scaling law for short-time dynamics, independent of microscopic details, provides a powerful diagnostic tool for characterizing memory effects in strongly correlated quantum gases.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses how strongly correlated quantum many-body systems behave when subjected to non-Markovian dissipation, capturing the interplay between strong correlations and memory effects.

**Main result.** The short-time dynamics of a system observable in a critical state universally follows a $t^{2\eta}$ scaling law, which then crosses over to either $t^{2\eta-1}$ or linear-in-$t$ behavior at longer times.

**Method.** The authors develop a general theoretical framework using linear response theory and perturbative expansions in the coupling strength ($J$) to calculate observable changes ($\delta W(t)$).

**Model / system.** The analysis uses general models of strongly interacting particles coupled to an environment, with specific examples including two-mode Bose systems and one-dimensional interacting Bose gases (Lieb–Liniger Hamiltonian).

**Key observables.** Change in observable ($\delta W(t)$), total atom number ($\hat{N}$), momentum occupation ($\hat{n}_k$), and spectral densities ($S^{(1)}, S^{(2)}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Critical exponent $\eta$, memory time $t_0 = 1/\Gamma$, dissipation time $t_d$, and the coupling strength $J$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory relies on perturbative expansions, a factorized two-frequency spectral density approximation, and assuming applicability of edge power laws over low-energy frequency windows.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare full master equation simulations against response theory predictions for conserved and nonconserved observables in Bose systems, illustrating the crossover from short-time quadratic/power-law growth to linear-in-$t$ behavior.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes a general theoretical framework for dissipation response, derives scaling laws for both conserved and nonconserved observables across different time regimes ($t \ll t_0$, $t_0 \ll t \ll t_d$), and applies these results to specific quantum gas models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Strongly interacting quantum many-body systems subjected to non-Markovian dissipation pose a formidable challenge due to the interplay between strong correlation effects and memory effects. In this Letter, we develop a general theoretical framework to compute how a system observable responds to dissipation, which captures memory effects at short times and recovers the Markovian limit at longer times. Using this framework, we predict that, for a strongly correlated quantum critical state with critical exponent $η$, the short-time dynamics of a system observable always obeys a $t^{2η}$ scaling law. This emerges as a universal result from the interplay between strong correlation and memory effects, independent of the microscopic Hamiltonian of the system. We further reveal a crossover behavior of this scaling law to either $t^{2η-1}$ or linear-in-$t$ behavior beyond the memory time scale. We propose a concrete physical realization of a non-Markovian bath with tunable memory time using ultracold atoms, where our predictions can be straightforwardly verified in current experiments.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes anomalous current fluctuations in the stochastic XNOR hopping model by formulating three conjectures describing the long-time limiting distributions of the integrated spin current. Using numerical simulations, the authors provide strong evidence supporting these predictions—Gaussian, half-normal, and M-Wright limits—for different initial magnetization regimes.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides concrete, non-trivial scaling laws for current fluctuations in a specific interacting quantum/classical spin model, which is highly relevant for understanding transport in low-dimensional quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates anomalous current fluctuations (Fixed-Cut Current Statistics) in one-dimensional many-body systems, specifically focusing on the stochastic XNOR hopping model.

**Main result.** Three distinct conjectures are proposed for the long-time scaling limits of the integrated spin current depending on the initial state: Gaussian ($t^{1/4}$), half-normal ($t^{1/4}$), and M-Wright ($t^{1/8}$) distributions.

**Method.** The analysis involves formulating theoretical conjectures based on microscopic dynamics, followed by numerical simulations to provide evidence for these predicted scaling laws and amplitudes.

**Model / system.** The system is the stochastic XNOR process, a kinetically constrained hopping model defined on a 1D chain of spins ($\eta_j$). The dynamics are governed by local exchange rules involving four-site blocks.

**Key observables.** The integrated spin current $J(t)$ through a fixed bond, and its rescaled moments $\mathbb{E}[(J(t)/t^\alpha)^r]$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Initial magnetization ($m$), time scaling exponents ($\alpha = 1/4$ or $1/8$), and the specific initial state (homogeneous vs. domain-wall).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on conjectures for the limiting probability distributions, and numerical support is provided by simulations; a separate companion paper contains an unverified candidate proof.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare Monte Carlo density histograms of the rescaled current against the predicted M-Wright density, showing agreement at large times and larger system volumes ($L=512, L=1024$).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem (Section 3), details analytic strategies by mapping to simpler processes (Section 4), and concludes with numerical tests supporting the conjectures (Section 5).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider fluctuations of the spin current in the stochastic XNOR process, a kinetically constrained hopping model in one spatial dimension. We formulate three conjectures with explicit amplitudes for the long-time current distributions: a Gaussian limit on the $t^{1/4}$ scale for homogeneous initial states with nonzero magnetization, a half-normal limit on the $t^{1/4}$ scale for domain-wall states with opposite magnetizations, and an M-Wright limit on the $t^{1/8}$ scale for homogeneous initial states at zero magnetization. Earlier work identified the tracer mechanism and anticipated the scaling exponents and limiting shapes in the domain-wall and zero-magnetization settings. Starting from the microscopic XNOR dynamics, we predict the missing amplitudes for the continuous-time process and extend the picture to homogeneous biased initial data. Simulations provide numerical support for all three conjectures without fitted parameters. A separate mathematical companion paper presents an extensively AI-generated candidate proof. Generative-AI tools were used in the research and writing workflow.

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents a practical and scalable method to generate resource states required for CV quantum computation by utilizing quantum dots in an optical cavity. The protocol generates finite-energy qunaught states through cat-breeding, overcoming the limitations of previous methods that suffered from low success rates due to strict measurement post-selection. This approach is highly relevant as it integrates solid-state physics with advanced photonic quantum information processing.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, chip-compatible roadmap for generating non-Gaussian resources essential for fault tolerance, bridging fundamental cavity QED physics (QD interaction) directly into quantum information processing protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is generating fault-tolerant Continuous Variable (CV) quantum computation resources, specifically GKP states, because their ideal form possesses unphysical infinite energy.

**Main result.** The authors propose a scalable protocol using quantum dots in a cavity to generate finite-energy qunaught states via cat-breeding, significantly improving success rates by relaxing strict post-selection criteria.

**Method.** The scheme combines deterministic squeezed cat-state generation from the QD-cavity system with multi-round cat-breeding protocols and homodyne measurements.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a strongly coupled quantum dot embedded in an optical cavity (Cavity-QED). The dynamics are modeled using cascaded-systems formalism to account for finite pulse durations and mode selectivity.

**Key observables.** Fidelity of the generated state, generation probability/success rate, Wigner distribution (showing negative values), and temporal correlation functions ($g^{(1)}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Squeezing parameter ($r$), cavity loss ($\kappa$), finite pulse duration, and the structured set of homodyne measurement outcomes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The ideal GKP states are unphysical, necessitating the use of finite-energy approximations like the qunaught state. The protocol assumes compatibility with integrated photonics and telecom wavelengths.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Wigner distribution of cat states, the three-cavity setup for simulation, and fidelity oscillations due to ground-state precession in non-degenerate systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews the state-of-the-art (comparing microwave vs. optical), details the cat-breeding protocol, formalizes the light-matter interaction Hamiltonian for the QD-cavity system, presents numerical simulations analyzing fidelity trade-offs, and concludes with a comparison to other GKP architectures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

GKP states enable fault-tolerant CV quantum computation, but their generation remains experimentally challenging due to their highly non-Gaussian and infinite-energy ideal structure. In this work, we present a realistic and scalable protocol for generating finite-energy resource states, specifically the qunaught state, using Schrodinger cat states generated in a strongly coupled quantum dot-cavity system. Our scheme combines deterministic squeezed cat-state generation, cat-breeding protocols, and homodyne measurements. Using numerical simulations, we analyze the role of various parameters of the quantum dot-cavity system in the generation of practical qunaught states. Furthermore, we quantify the trade-off between the fidelity and generation probability of these states and exploit the fact that accepting a structured set of homodyne outcomes can significantly enhance the overall success rate. The proposed approach is compatible with integrated photonics and telecom wavelengths, offering a promising route toward scalable CV quantum information processing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper models the spectroscopic response of SiV- centers in diamond under strain using MDCS theory. By simulating measurements under different detection schemes (heterodyne vs. PL), the authors determined that random anisotropic strain best describes spectral broadening. The comparison between techniques successfully separates radiative from nonradiative decay pathways, providing critical insights for quantum sensing applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses open quantum system dynamics in solid-state emitters, using advanced spectroscopy to map out environmental coupling (strain) and identify non-radiative loss channels, which is central to building reliable quantum memories and sensors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to understand how strain variations and dark states affect the spectroscopic measurements of silicon-vacancy (SiV-) centers in diamond when using advanced techniques like optical multidimensional coherent spectroscopy (MDCS).

**Main result.** Fully random anisotropic strain best reproduces the measured spectral broadening, yielding characteristic axial and shear strain values. Furthermore, comparing heterodyne vs. photoluminescence detection separates radiative from nonradiative dynamics.

**Method.** The authors developed a computational model using density-matrix perturbation theory within a Bloch-model framework, employing Monte Carlo sampling to average over random strain configurations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the negatively charged SiV- center in diamond. The model simulates MDCS signals by calculating spectral pole positions ($	ilde{\Omega}_{ab}$) that depend explicitly on six components of the local strain tensor ($\epsilon$).

**Key observables.** Two-dimensional amplitude spectra $|	ext{Stot}(
u_t, 
u_	au)|$, spectral inhomogeneity, and the ratio between heterodyne and photoluminescence signals.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Axial strain variation ($2.8 	imes 10^{-4}$), shear strain variation ($3.5 	imes 10^{-5}$), and the nonradiative decay rate ($\Gamma_{	ext{nr}}$) relative to radiative rates.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses approximations like the impulsive limit and Markovian limit; a major limitation is that reproducing PL spectra requires an extremely high, potentially unphysical, ratio of nonradiative to radiative rates.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the MDCS setup for both detection schemes (heterodyne vs. PL), the four-level energy structure of SiV-, and comparisons showing how fully random anisotropic strain best fits measured spectral lineshapes compared to structured strain models.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the theoretical framework using density matrix theory, then applying Monte Carlo sampling to model ensemble averaging over strain distributions, and finally comparing the predictions from different detection schemes (heterodyne vs. PL) to constrain physical parameters like strain magnitude and decay pathways.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Negatively charged silicon-vacancy (SiV$^-$) centers in diamond offer an attractive platform for the development of many forms of quantum technology. However, questions remain in connection to how large ensembles of SiV$^-$ centers behave in concert. Here, we develop a computational model designed to simulate recent experiments where optical multidimensional coherent spectroscopy (MDCS) was used to examine a high-concentration sample of SiV$^-$ centers in diamond, revealing significant variations in spectral signature depending on the detection scheme. Simulation results reveal that strain effects are highly random in this system, with a characteristic axial strain of $2.8 \times 10^{-4}$ and a shear strain of $3.5 \times 10^{-5}$. They suggest in addition that highly strained centers (with values exceeding $1.5 \times 10^{-5}$) may become significantly decoupled from optical emission. The results have implications for the use of SiV$^-$ centers as quantum sensors.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the topology of generalized dissipative Majorana wires using a Lindblad master equation framework. By calculating spectral gaps and defining a winding number invariant, the authors show that this topology persists even with damping. They demonstrate that engineering couplings allows control over the topological state, achieving higher bound states than simpler models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges non-equilibrium quantum dynamics (Lindblad equations) with topological concepts typically associated with Hermitian Hamiltonians. The ability to define robust topological invariants in the presence of dissipation is highly relevant for understanding open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates topological states within generalized one-dimensional (1D) quantum dissipative models, specifically those modeling Majorana wires that can host multiple bound states.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that a winding number serves as a robust topological invariant distinguishing steady states even when damping and purity gaps are present. Furthermore, incorporating next-nearest-neighbor couplings allows for achieving higher winding numbers.

**Method.** The analysis utilizes the adjoint Lindblad equation applied to fermionic two-point correlations, calculating spectral gaps (damping and purity) and defining topology via a symmetry-based winding number calculation.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as a 1D fermionic open quantum system whose dynamics are governed by a quadratic Lindblad equation. The model generalizes the Kitaev chain by including imbalanced couplings and next-nearest-neighbor interactions.

**Key observables.** Damping gap, purity gap, and the topological winding number ($
u_{1D}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strengths ($\kappa, 	heta, eta$), which control the closing/opening of the dissipative gap.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on transforming to a mean-field description using phase-conserving linear operators and analyzing steady states derived from fermionic correlations rather than direct Majorana operator evolution.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of the dissipative gap ($\Delta_d$) and the topological winding number ($
u$) on coupling parameters, showing that changes in $
u$ coincide with the closing of $\Delta_d$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the mathematical framework using Lindblad dynamics for fermionic correlations, deriving the topological invariant (winding number), and then applying this analysis to specific generalized models (Model I and Model II) to map out their phase diagrams.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the generalized one-dimensional (1D) quantum dissipative models corresponding to a Majorana wire which can possess more than one Majorana bound state at each end. The system consists of a 1D fermionic open quantum system whose dynamics is governed by a quadratic Lindblad equation. Using the adjoint Lindblad equation for the fermionic two-point correlations, we find the gaps in the damping and purity spectra of a generic 1D model. Then, using the symmetry-based classification, we show that a winding number as the topological invariant can be defined which distinguishes different steady states of the system in the presence of damping and purity gaps. Then we focus on certain models with different Lindblad quantum jump terms and explore their phase diagrams by calculating the damping and the purity gaps as well as the winding number. In particular, we show that by inclusion of quantum jumps between next-nearest-neighbor sites, higher winding numbers and, equivalently, more Majorana bound states can be achieved. Also, by introducing imbalanced couplings, we can switch between states with negative and positive winding numbers. Finally, we should mention that since our formulation is based on the fermionic correlations rather than the Majorana operators, it can be easily extended to the dissipative topological phases belonging to other symmetry classes.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops an advanced numerical technique, extending TDNRG, to calculate the work distribution function for strongly coupled open quantum systems like quantum dots. By analyzing quenches, the authors reveal universal scaling laws and power-law thresholds in the WDF, confirming deep connections between non-equilibrium thermodynamics and impurity physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a numerically exact, non-perturbative tool (TDNRG) to analyze fundamental quantities in non-equilibrium quantum thermodynamics, directly addressing the complex physics of strong coupling and dissipation relevant to AMO and open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study focuses on determining the quantum work distribution function (WDF) for non-equilibrium processes in strongly coupled many-body open quantum systems following a sudden quench.

**Main result.** The authors reveal power-law threshold behavior near minimum work, linked to the Anderson orthogonality catastrophe, and demonstrate universal scaling collapse below an emergent low-energy scale.

**Method.** They extend the time-dependent numerical renormalization group (TDNRG) approach to calculate the WDF directly in the thermodynamic limit.

**Model / system.** The work is applied to quantum impurity models, specifically citing the Anderson Impurity Model (AIM) and the Spin-Boson Model (SBM), which describe system degrees of freedom interacting with gapless baths.

**Key observables.** Quantum Work Distribution Function ($P(W)$), Jarzynski's equality verification, work threshold behavior, and universal scaling collapse.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Strong coupling regime, quench amplitudes (zero or finite temperature), low-energy scale induced by system-bath correlations, and the bath spectral density ($\propto \omega$ for Ohmic baths).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method is non-perturbative, necessary because previous studies often relied on approximations that fail in strongly coupled regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates AIM (quantum dot) and SBM (dissipative tunneling). Figure 2 shows WDFs for level quenches in AIM demonstrating scaling collapse. Figure 3 presents TDNRG results for work at finite temperatures, verifying fluctuation theorems.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the extension of TDNRG to compute $P(W)$ for generalized quantum impurity models following a quench, analyzing key diagnostics like power-law thresholds and universal scaling collapse across different temperature regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The non-equilibrium quantum thermodynamics of many-body open systems is notoriously rich, especially in the strong-coupling regime where strong system-bath correlations develop and interactions produce non-perturbative effects. Such systems can be described by `quantum impurity models' where the system and bath are treated on an equal footing as a single composite. Paradigmatic examples are the spin-boson and Anderson impurity models, in which system degrees of freedom interact with either bosonic or fermionic gapless baths. Here we study the quantum work distribution function (WDF) of such systems following a quench. Capturing the full continuum of many-body excitations in the WDF requires a non-perturbative solution of the underlying non-equilibrium quantum impurity problem. For this purpose, we extend the time-dependent numerical renormalization group (TDNRG) approach to the calculation of the WDF, which applies directly in the thermodynamic limit, can be used for arbitrary quench amplitudes at zero or finite temperature, and provides exponentially fine low-energy resolution. Our numerically-exact solution reveals power-law threshold behavior due to the Anderson orthogonality catastrophe when the work approaches its minimum value, with universal scaling collapse of the distribution below an emergent low-energy scale induced by system-bath correlations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes the jamming transition in an active 1D particle system where particles self-propel and randomly reverse direction. Using Monte Carlo simulations and Mean Field Theory, the authors show that activity actively resists jamming, causing the critical density for jamming to drop as activity increases. This suggests a fundamental control mechanism over phase transitions using external driving forces.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides theoretical insights into how non-equilibrium driving forces modify fundamental phase transitions, which has parallels in understanding active matter systems relevant to biological or synthetic physical contexts.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how introducing activity (self-propulsion with switching) affects the jamming transition, a key non-equilibrium phase transition in passive particle systems.

**Main result.** Activity generally hinders or can completely inhibit the formation of a jammed state; specifically, the critical density for jamming decreases as the switching rate decreases, potentially keeping the system fluid even at high densities.

**Method.** The research employs both Monte Carlo simulations to study time evolution and Mean Field Theory (MFT) to derive steady-state equations for particle distributions and mean mobility.

**Model / system.** The model is a one-dimensional ring of hard core particles where each particle has an internal binary variable ($\eta = \pm 1$) determining its self-propulsion direction, which switches at rate $	ilde{\gamma}$.

**Key observables.** Mean mobility ($w$), the critical jamming density ($ho_c$), and the distribution functions of hole clusters ($P^{\alphaeta}_n$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Particle density ($ho$), switching rate ($	ilde{\gamma}$ or $\gamma$), and a hopping parameter $b$. Key regimes include the passive limit ($	ilde{\gamma} 	o \infty$) and the small activity limit ($	ilde{\gamma} 	o 0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The Mean Field Theory approach ignores spatial correlations between sites, and certain analyses rely on perturbation theory or specific scaling limits.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate mean particle mobility ($w$) versus density ($ho$) and switching rate ($\gamma$), showing agreement with passive models at high $\gamma$ and summarizing the phase diagram (Fluid/Jammed).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the active system dynamics to applying MFT for steady-state analysis. It then analyzes limiting cases: large $\gamma$ (approaching passive behavior), small $\gamma$ (high activity), and finally, a scaling limit ($ho/\gamma$ finite) to derive asymptotic behaviors.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Multiple studies on active matter have shown that activity can induce or suppress a phase transition, or modify the critical behavior of a passive system. Here we investigate how activity affects the jamming transition which is a paradigmatic example of nonequilibrium phase transitions in passive systems. We consider a one-dimensional system of active particles with hardcore interactions and a direction of self-propulsion which can be reversed at a given switching rate. For a class of particle hop rates and infinite switching rate, our model reduces to a passive system which is known to exhibit a transition between a high-density fluid phase and a low-density jammed phase in the stationary state. Using Monte Carlo simulations and a mean field theory, we study how the mean mobility of the particle and the hole cluster distribution vary with density and finite switching rate. Our main result is that activity hinders the formation of jam and can even inhibit it; more precisely, we find that the jamming transition occurs at a critical density that decreases with decreasing switching rate, and at sufficiently small switching rate, the system exists only in the fluid phase.

</details>

**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `quantum measurements` **4/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **3/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **3/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5**

**Summary.** This paper develops rigorous bounds on apparent violations of the Second Law in quantum trajectories using advanced fluctuation theorems. By defining a completed entropy $\Omega$, it shows that the probability of observing negative entropy production ($\sigma < 0$) is quantitatively constrained by this completed observable's mean value. This provides a deep theoretical understanding of how measurement and environment coupling enforce thermodynamic constraints.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful theoretical tool for quantifying non-equilibrium thermodynamics in open quantum systems by linking apparent violations to measurable dynamical asymmetries, which is crucial for understanding measurement backaction and irreversible processes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes rigorous bounds for apparent violations of the Second Law of Thermodynamics, specifically concerning negative stochastic entropy production in quantum trajectories.

**Main result.** It derives a quantitative lower bound ($\Pi_\sigma \ge L(\langle\Omegaangle)$) showing that apparent violations are constrained by the completed entropy and dynamical asymmetry, even when standard fluctuation theorems do not strictly apply to $\sigma$ alone.

**Method.** The analysis employs path reversal techniques, general arbitrary-coupling formulations, and transfers established fluctuation theorem floors from a completed observable ($\Omega$) to the physical sign statistic ($\Pi_\sigma$).

**Model / system.** The theory is applied to quantum trajectories describing open evolution, modeled using random finite-coupling collision ensembles or coherently driven qubits interacting with thermal ancillas.

**Key observables.** Physical entropy production ($\sigma$), dynamical-asymmetry term ($\sigma^*$), completed entropy ($\Omega$), and the tie-corrected physical sign statistic ($\Pi_\sigma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The bound depends on the mean of the completed observable, $\langle\Omegaangle$, which controls the severity of the constraint.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on the path law satisfying a product identity and the general applicability of fluctuation theorems to the completed observable $\Omega$.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the derived inequality ($\Pi_\sigma \ge \Pi_\Omega \ge L(\langle\Omegaangle)$) using results from both random collision models and coherently driven qubit systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem of apparent violations, develops the mathematical framework relating $\sigma$ to $\Omega$, proves the optimality of the likelihood-ratio sign, derives the core inequality ($\Pi_\sigma \ge L(\langle\Omegaangle)$), and finally validates this result using two distinct physical models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Negative stochastic entropy production is commonly called an apparent violation of the second law. In general quantum-trajectory dynamics, however, the physical entropy production $σ$ need not obey a forward detailed fluctuation theorem. A general arbitrary-coupling formulation identifies a dynamical-asymmetry term $σ^\ast$ that completes it into $Ω=σ+σ^\ast$, whose mean is $\langleΩ\rangle=Σ+Σ^\ast$. We prove that the likelihood-ratio sign is optimal among reversal-odd trajectory observables and use this fact to transfer an established sharp fluctuation-theorem floor to the tie-corrected physical sign statistic $Π_σ=\Pr(σ<0)+\Pr(σ=0)/2$. When $\Pr(σ=0)=0$, the result reads $\Pr(σ<0)\ge[1-\langleΩ\rangle/g(\langleΩ\rangle)]/2$, where $g$ is the inverse of $a\mapsto a\tanh(a/2)$. The physical integral fluctuation theorem simultaneously suppresses large negative events, producing a quantitative ``frequent but mild'' law, while the sign imbalance lower-bounds the hidden mean $Σ^\ast$. We formulate the measured-record protocol explicitly and illustrate and numerically audit the tie-corrected theorem in random finite-coupling collision models and a coherently driven qubit interacting with thermal ancillas.

</details>

**Summary.** The research experimentally realizes a specific quantum subgraph within a quantum random network using an integrated silicon photonic chip. By exploiting entangled photon pairs and local operations, the authors demonstrate connectivity patterns achievable only through quantum entanglement, validating predictions from QRN theory over classical models.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete experimental evidence in photonics that harnesses genuine multipartite entanglement to engineer complex quantum structures predicted by advanced network theories, bridging fundamental theory with scalable hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to experimentally realize a quantum subgraph structure predicted by quantum random network theory, contrasting this with classical connectivity thresholds.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated the preparation of a specific finite quantum subgraph ($\lambda$ graph) using an integrated photonic chip, confirming that entanglement enables connectivity structures inaccessible classically.

**Method.** The experiment uses probabilistic photon-pair sources and local transformations on an integrated silicon photonic chip, followed by mode-selective postselection to extract the desired entangled state.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is an integrated silicon photonic chip utilizing spontaneous four-wave mixing for generating Bell-state links. The theoretical framework compares classical random graphs (Erdős–Rényi) with Quantum Random Networks (QRN).

**Key observables.** Fidelity ($F_{\exp}$), entanglement witness values ($W$), and visibility measurements (RHOM/HHOM contrast/visibility).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The experiment operates in a regime distinct from the large-$N$ threshold scaling, focusing on finite subgraph realization.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocol relies on probabilistic photon-pair sources and postselection; the fidelity calculations assume measurement capability across all required projections.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup using integrated photonic circuits, schematic protocols for state preparation (e.g., four-node module), and interference patterns used for characterization.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical difference between classical and quantum network connectivity to detailing the physical implementation on a silicon chip, followed by rigorous measurements of entanglement dimensionality using fidelity and visibility witnesses.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding how complex connectivity emerges in networks is a fundamental challenge in classical and quantum science. In classical random networks, complex subgraphs typically require relatively high connection probabilities, whereas quantum random network theory predicts that such structures can arise at a single, lower threshold through entanglement and local operations. Here, using an integrated silicon photonic chip, we experimentally realize a quantum subgraph predicted by quantum random network theory in a four-node quantum random network. Our integrated platform exploits probabilistic photon-pair sources and coherent control of path modes to prepare a structured quantum subgraph through local transformations and postselection, operating in a threshold regime that differs from classical random networks. We verify that the subgraph state exhibits genuine high-dimensional multipartite entanglement across the nodes, providing experimental evidence that quantum entanglement enables connectivity structures beyond classical accessibility.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes a method using free-electron-photon interactions to perform nondestructive projection measurements on the electron comb basis. This technique overcomes the limitations of traditional destructive measurements by coupling the electron state to auxiliary optical modes. The results demonstrate that this setup can be used for quantum error mitigation and detecting entanglement (EPR steering) between the electron qubit and the photon field.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges condensed matter physics (free electrons) with advanced quantum optics techniques (squeezing, homodyne detection) to establish a novel platform for non-destructive quantum state characterization and computation in solid-state systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Conventional measurements on free electrons are destructive, limiting their use as quantum qubits. The goal is to perform nondestructive projection measurement on the electron comb basis.

**Main result.** A proposed scheme (FECBPM) allows for nondestructive measurement of the electron comb basis and demonstrates its utility in quantum error mitigation and witnessing EPR steering between electrons and photons.

**Method.** The method involves coupling free electrons to optical modes via PINEM interactions, followed by projection measurement using orthogonal squeezed vacuum states and homodyne detection on the auxiliary optical mode.

**Model / system.** The system is a free electron interacting with photon modes in a Near-field Electron Microscopy (PINEM) setup. The interaction Hamiltonian governs the coupling, leading to an effective scattering operator that describes state evolution.

**Key observables.** Fidelity of optical modes ($ho_i^{ph}$), violation of EPR steering inequalities ($\Delta^2_{	ext{inf}X}\Delta^2_{	ext{inf}P} < 1/4$), and the measurable phase shift $\delta\phi$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strength ($g$), squeezing parameter ($r$), and error parameters like propagation distance ($\phi$) or thermal photon occupation ($ar{n}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on comparing optical state fidelities rather than direct electron state fidelity due to mathematical constraints; the use of squeezed vacuum states approximates ideal quadrature measurements.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental scheme using squeezing angles, compare measured vs. ideal optical state fidelities (Wigner functions), show probability distributions before and after error mitigation, and demonstrate EPR steering detection dependence on coupling strength and thermal noise.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by first defining the electron-photon interaction Hamiltonian and scattering operator; it then details the conditions for reliable projection measurement via homodyne detection; subsequently, it applies this to quantum error correction and finally analyzes EPR steering criteria.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Free electrons, driven by rapid advances in photon-induced near-field electron microscopy, have emerged as a promising platform for quantum information processing, including quantum computing and quantum sensing. However, conventional measurements that rely on the electron energy loss spectrum (EELS) are inherently destructive to electron qubits, thereby constraining their applicability. In this Letter, we propose a scheme that performs projection measurement on the electron comb basis, where high measurement precision can be achieved with bright squeezed vacuum states and strong PINEM couplings. Notably, this approach is not only nondestructive to electron qubits but also maximally incompatible with energy measurements, enabling alternative quantum information applications, such as quantum error mitigation and Einstein-Podolsky-Rosen steering detection. Our findings open an avenue towards a systematic understanding of quantum free electrons and towards the development of nondestructive free electron quantum information tasks.

</details>

**Summary.** The research reports achieving single-qubit gate fidelities above 99.999% in silicon spin qubits by addressing decoherence mechanisms inherent to microwave driving. Key findings pinpoint that proximal reservoirs degrade the critical $T_{1ho}$ coherence time, and using spectrally tailored Gaussian pulses minimizes benchmarking artifacts. This work establishes practical limits for high-fidelity quantum operations in solid-state systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides crucial experimental guidelines for scaling quantum computation by quantifying how specific noise sources—like reservoir coupling and off-resonant excitation—limit gate performance in solid-state qubits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is achieving single-qubit gate fidelities exceeding 99.999% in driven silicon spin qubits while rigorously identifying the physical mechanisms that limit this performance.

**Main result.** High fidelities were achieved by mitigating decoherence sources, particularly by optimizing device conditions to suppress off-resonant driving effects and extending the spin-locking coherence time ($T_{1ho}$).

**Method.** The study employs randomized benchmarking (RB) and purity measurements alongside detailed characterization of various coherence times ($T_2^*$, $T_{1ho}$) under microwave driving.

**Model / system.** The platform is semiconductor single-spin qubits realized in silicon quantum dots, controlled via Electric Dipole Spin Resonance (EDSR). The analysis models noise sources arising from proximal reservoirs and off-resonant microwave excitation.

**Key observables.** Single-qubit gate fidelity ($\pi/2$ rotation), spin-locking coherence time ($T_{1ho}$), $T_2^*$ echo decay, and frequency shifts due to heating.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate fidelity target $>99.999\%$; reservoir proximity distance ($d$); Rabi frequency ($f_{	ext{Rabi}}$); temperature ($T_{	ext{MXC}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The residual error is primarily limited by incoherent noise, and the analysis models capacitive coupling ($\eta(d)$) between the qubit and external reservoirs.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate SEM images of the device, measurement sequences (PSB readout), spin resonance spectra, coherence time measurements ($T_{1ho}$ vs. reservoir proximity), and fidelity decay plots comparing RB and purity results for different pulse shapes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing basic qubit characterization (coherence times) in the presence of varying environmental couplings (reservoirs). It then investigates systematic errors from microwave driving (pulse shaping, heating) before concluding with optimized protocols that achieve state-of-the-art fidelity benchmarks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In semiconductor single-spin qubits, high-fidelity quantum gates have been demonstrated; however, achieving consistent performance remains challenging due to variations in driven qubit coherence, which is less explored than free-evolution coherence such as $T_2^*$. Here, we report single-qubit gate fidelities above 99.999%, achieved by dramatically extending the driven-spin coherence time and suppressing off-resonant driving effects that are detrimental to accurate fidelity benchmarking. We demonstrate that removing proximal reservoirs significantly enhances the spin-locking coherence time ($T_{1ρ}$), a critical metric for qubits under microwave driving. Furthermore, we reveal that in typical spin qubit setups using parity readout and rectangular pulses, off-resonant excitation of neighboring qubits causes substantial benchmarking artifacts. By optimizing device conditions to mitigate microwave-induced degradation and implementing spectrally tailored pulse shaping, we achieve a $π/2$ gate fidelity of 99.99920(2)%, with remaining errors primarily limited by incoherent noise. These results showcase the mechanisms that bound fidelity benchmarking in state-of-the-art silicon spin qubits and provide practical guidelines for achieving and verifying high fidelities in these systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper experimentally reveals that superconducting transmon qubits suffer from memory effects caused by slow charge fluctuations within the material's defect bath. By tuning the electric field, the authors measured hysteretic changes in qubit decay rates with timescales reaching seconds. This work provides vital insights into non-Markovian noise sources, which is critical for advancing robust quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses noise sources in superconducting qubits by characterizing non-Markovian noise from defect baths, providing crucial data for improving qubit stabilization and quantum error correction protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates long-lived memory effects originating from defect baths coupled to superconducting qubits, which introduce detrimental temporally correlated noise.

**Main result.** The research observed memory timescales on the order of seconds, significantly exceeding typical coherence times, attributed to coupling with electric field-polarized charge fluctuators (CFs).

**Method.** The authors used electric-field tuning via a gate electrode and performed fast, repeated measurements of qubit relaxation probability ($P_e$) over extended laboratory time.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a superconducting transmon qubit coupled to microscopic Two-Level System (TLS) defects. The memory effects are modeled by coupling the TLSs to electric field-polarized charge fluctuators (CFs).

**Key observables.** Qubit relaxation probability ($P_e$), hysteretic behavior in TLS resonance voltages, and sweep-rate dependence of $P_e$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Memory timescales ($	au \sim$ seconds), electric field tuning voltage ($V_g$), and the characteristic equilibration time ($	au_{eq} \approx 5.4$ s).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the qubit transition frequency is insensitive to offset charge, and the CF model uses an approximation near the symmetry point.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental protocol, showing $P_e$ vs. sweep time over long lab times, comparing increasing/decreasing sweeps to reveal hysteresis, and demonstrating sweep-rate dependence of the measured probabilities.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the experimental setup using electric-field tuning, presents measurements showing hysteretic behavior indicative of memory effects, models these effects using a charge-fluctuator approach, and quantifies the long equilibration times observed.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We reveal long-lived memory effects in the defect bath of a superconducting transmon qubit through electric-field tuning of two-level system (TLS) defects coupled to the qubit. Using a fast TLS mapping method we observe several hysteretic effects in the TLS environment with memory timescales of the order of seconds, far beyond the lifetimes of individual TLS defects. The observations can be explained by TLS coupling to electric field-polarised charge fluctuators in the defect bath. Our method enables detailed mapping of the dynamics of the bath's coupled microscopic degrees of freedom and the associated memory effects which can introduce temporally correlated noise. This information may be used to improve qubit-stabilisation and quantum error correction protocols.

</details>

**Authors:** Yousef K. Chahine, Chaohan Cui, William DeRocco, Daniel Gottesman, Saikat Guha, Emil T. Khabiboulline, Zhenning Liu, Brittany McClinton, Jayadev Rajagopal, Fredrik Rantakyro, J. Gabriel Richardson, Stephen Ridgway, Aqil Sajjad, Joohyung Song

Based on analysis of a spectrally-filtered weak thermal field loaded into a sequence of temporally-gated quantum memories.</figcaption>

**Summary.** This paper outlines a revolutionary method for long-baseline optical interferometry that replaces the need to physically transport light over kilometers by storing and processing photon coherence within quantum memories. By mapping the astronomical signal onto memory states, it fundamentally changes the stability requirements from maintaining path length accuracy to controlling local timing and phase references. This advances the field by merging quantum information techniques with cutting-edge astrophysics instrumentation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it applies advanced concepts from quantum information (entanglement distribution, memory storage) to solve a major problem in experimental quantum optics/astrophysics. The shift in required stability metrics provides new theoretical frameworks for analyzing complex quantum measurement protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the fundamental limitations of classical long-baseline optical interferometry for astrophysical measurements by proposing a quantum memory-based approach.

**Main result.** Using quantum memories allows the measurement of photon coherences across distant sites without requiring the physical transport and recombination of light over vast distances, shifting stability concerns from path length to local timing control.

**Method.** The method involves mapping the coherent superposition state of incoming photons onto a collective quantum memory state using entanglement-assisted operations. Coherence is then interrogated via joint measurements on the stored quantum states.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is an optical interferometer utilizing multiple distant telescopes (A, B, etc.) whose light signals are captured and processed within solid-state or atomic quantum memories. The process relies on entanglement to correlate information across these separated nodes.

**Key observables.** Mutual coherence ($G_{AB}(t)$), complex visibility, and the reconstructed fringe term which depends on residual differential delay ($\delta	au(t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coherence time ($	au_c$), memory bin width ($t_w$), synchronization error ($	au$), and the required phase stability for local oscillators.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that quantum memories can successfully capture, store, and allow joint measurement of the complex superposition state of incoming photons from multiple sources. It also assumes established techniques for phase referencing remain necessary.

**Paper structure.** The paper first contrasts classical interferometry limitations with quantum memory approaches, detailing how the stability requirement shifts. It then describes the core mechanism of state transfer into memories and discusses the reconstruction of visibility via joint measurements on stored states, concluding with a discussion of remaining technological hurdles.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this paper, we describe the fundamental operating mechanisms of optical interferometry using quantum memory and entanglement. We show how these remove the optical delay line bottleneck. Quantum memory is not without its own set of challenges, some of which include very small bandwidths as well as limitations in storage time. We examine the influence of timing artifacts on memory photon capture probability and interferometric complex visibility. We highlight keystone areas of technology that require further development and are essential to realizing these opportunities, as well as ongoing work to overcome these challenges.

</details>
