**Summary.** This work presents an AI-enabled framework that tackles the difficult inverse problem of determining quantum magnetic interactions from experimental spectra. By treating different measurements (single-crystal vs. powder INS) as geometric constraints on the parameter space, it quantifies which interactions are uniquely identifiable and guides adaptive data collection strategies for improved characterization.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous quantification of parameter identifiability via geometric constraints provides a powerful systematic tool for guiding future experiments in strongly correlated materials, moving beyond simple fitting to understanding measurement limitations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is determining microscopic magnetic interactions (Hamiltonian parameters) from complex, multimodal spectroscopic data, as the inverse problem is often ill-posed.

**Main result.** The proposed framework successfully performs uncertainty-aware Hamiltonian inference by quantifying how different measurement modalities constrain the parameter space using observation geometry.

**Method.** It employs an AI surrogate model (INRs) trained on simulated spectra to map Hamiltonian parameters to measured spectral data, integrating this with Bayesian inference.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on quantum magnets, specifically $	ext{NiPS}_3$, modeled by a spin Hamiltonian involving exchange couplings and anisotropies. Measurements utilize Inelastic Neutron Scattering (INS) from both single-crystal and powder samples.

**Key observables.** Dynamical structure factor $S(Q, \omega)$, inferred Hamiltonian parameters ($	heta$), spectral distinguishability metrics, and the local/global Gram matrix characterizing parameter sensitivity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hamiltonian coupling constants ($J_{ij}, A_x, A_z$) defining the magnetic interactions; momentum transfer $\mathbf{Q}$ and energy $\omega$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes that spectral distinguishability is governed by the geometry induced by the forward map ($	heta ightarrow S$), rather than Euclidean distance in parameter space. Source separation for powder data introduces model-dependent estimation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup of INS measurements (single-crystal vs. powder), the AI surrogate architecture, and detailed spectral discrepancy maps showing how different measurement modalities constrain the Hamiltonian parameters via geometry analysis.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the ill-posed nature of inverse problems in quantum magnetism. It then introduces the AI framework using INRs to model spectra from a given Hamiltonian. The core methodology involves characterizing parameter uncertainty through observation geometry (Gram matrix) and demonstrating its application on $	ext{NiPS}_3$ data, culminating in adaptive experimental design strategies.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Determining microscopic interactions from spectroscopic and scattering measurements is central to understanding quantum materials, yet it often remains unclear which interactions can be reliably revealed by the available experimental data and how additional experimental modalities should be designed to resolve the remaining ambiguities. Here we present an artificial intelligence-enabled framework for uncertainty-aware Hamiltonian inference and adaptive experimental design. By combining Hamiltonian-conditioned neural surrogates with Bayesian inference and observation geometry, the framework characterizes how measurements constrain Hamiltonian parameter space, quantifies the identifiability of microscopic interactions, and propagates posterior uncertainty directly in the physical Hamiltonian parameter space rather than an abstract learned representation. Using multimodal powder and single-crystal inelastic neutron scattering measurements of the quantum magnet NiPS$_{3}$, we demonstrate physically interpretable Hamiltonian inference, modality-aware uncertainty quantification, and adaptive experimental design. The framework provides a general strategy for uncertainty-aware microscopic characterization and multimodal experimental design across quantum materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically explains how high-temperature superconductivity can emerge in systems with flat energy bands, even when repulsive interactions are dominant. It proposes a 'self-help' mechanism where the superconducting pairing itself deforms the band structure, ensuring that supercurrents and superfluid weight remain finite. The findings align well with experimental observations in heavy-fermion and high-$T_c$ superconductors.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of a superconducting state actively modifying its own underlying electronic structure (band deformation) is a rich topic relevant to understanding emergent order parameters in complex quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how flat bands can induce high-temperature superconductivity, even when repulsive pairing interactions are present.

**Main result.** Superconductivity in the presence of flat bands is sustained by a 'self-help' mechanism where the superconducting state deforms the band, preventing the superfluid weight and supercurrent from vanishing.

**Method.** The analysis adapts standard BCS theory formalism to the specific physical context of flat bands formed in strongly correlated Fermi systems.

**Model / system.** The system involves strongly correlated Fermi gases exhibiting topological fermion condensation quantum phase transitions (FCQPT), leading to localized states forming flat bands at the chemical potential.

**Key observables.** Critical temperature ($T_c$), superfluid weight ($D_s$), effective mass ($M^*$), superconducting gap ($\Delta$), and Fermi velocity ($V_F$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Repulsive pairing interaction strength ($\lambda_0 > 0$); the condition for flat bands ($\varepsilon(p) - \mu = 0$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the superconducting state actively modifies the band structure (tilting it) to maintain finite superfluid properties, overcoming initial theoretical predictions of collapse.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows experimental data for $V_F$ vs. $T_c$ in graphene and $\Delta$ vs. integrated LDOS ($\propto 1/V_F$) for high-$T_c$ superconductors, both consistent with the derived theoretical relationship.

**Paper structure.** The paper first addresses the apparent contradiction where flat bands suggest zero superfluid weight; it then introduces the 'self-help' mechanism via BCS formalism to show finite $D_s$; finally, it discusses how this framework explains high-$T_c$ behavior even with repulsive interactions and relates findings to experimental data.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this letter, we examine how the presence of flat band leads to the formation of a high-temperature superconductor even in the case of repulsive pairing interactions. We also show that in the case of flat bands, the high-temperature superconducting state deforms the flat band, tilting it and making the effective mass finite. As a result, neither the superfluid weight nor the supercurrent disappear. Our results are in good agreement with experimental data.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops the Williamson majorization theory for fermionic non-Gaussianity, creating a resource measure analogous to entanglement measures in quantum information. It establishes that this spectral law governs state conversion under Gaussian protocols, providing strong monotonicity theorems and deriving irreversible bounds on how much non-Gaussian correlation can be converted between states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, quantifiable resource theory framework for fermionic quantum computation that directly links spectral properties (Williamson spectrum) to fundamental physical processes like state transformation and irreversible dynamics. The connection between entanglement monotones and non-Gaussianity is highly relevant.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes a majorization law for fermionic non-Gaussianity, drawing an analogy to how majorization governs entanglement under local operations.

**Main result.** It proves that the Williamson spectrum obeys a fundamental majorization law, turning convex deficits into strong monotones and establishing irreversible conversion bounds for pure states.

**Method.** The analysis uses spectral theory applied to Majorana covariance matrices, defining non-Gaussianity via the deviation of the Williamson spectrum from its Gaussian limit.

**Model / system.** The system is fermionic, described by $N$ Majorana operators. The diagnostics rely on the covariance matrix $\Gamma_ho$, which characterizes the state's correlations.

**Key observables.** Williamson Spectrum ($r_j$), Fermionic Non-Gaussianity ($\Phi_f$), Gaussian Nullity ($
u_G$), and conversion rates $R(\psi 	o \phi)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The function $f$ defining the monotone must be convex on $[0, 1]$ and satisfy $f(r) < f(1)$ for $r < 1$. Parity conservation is a key constraint.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theory primarily analyzes Gaussian protocols with pure state outcomes; rate bounds rely on asymptotic limits ($\epsilon_n 	o 0$) and definite fermionic parity.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the Williamson majorization law for pure states (analogous to entanglement) to characterizing non-Gaussianity monotones via convex functions $f$. It then extends this to single-shot and asymptotic conversion rates, proving irreversibility when parity is conserved.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Pure-state entanglement rests on a single algebraic backbone: majorization of the Schmidt spectrum governs state conversion under local operations and classical communication, and constrains entanglement monotones. Here we establish a corresponding majorization law for fermionic non-Gaussianity, the resource that elevates free fermions to universal quantum computation. Under any fermionic Gaussian protocol with pure state outcomes, the Williamson spectrum of a pure state's Majorana covariance matrix is weakly majorized by its ensemble average. This spectral law mirrors that of entanglement theory. It turns computable non-Gaussianity quantifiers such as fermionic antiflatness and occupation entropies into strong monotones for fermionic non-Gaussianity, and delivers necessary conditions and converse bounds on state conversion under Gaussian protocols. When fermion parity is conserved, no catalyst can remove a majorization obstruction---unless it carries parity coherence---and asymptotic interconversion is irreversible already for pure states. All relevant quantities are accessible from two-point Majorana correlators, turning the theory developed here into experimentally observable properties of quantum matter, testable on present-day quantum devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a full quantum-mechanical theory for two-electron Hanbury Brown-Twiss interference. It calculates the correlation function by treating both fermionic exchange statistics and Coulomb repulsion simultaneously. The results establish clear, measurable signatures that allow experimentalists to disentangle these two fundamental quantum effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous theoretical tool to distinguish between purely quantum mechanical effects (like fermionic antibunching) and classical/semiclassical interactions (like Coulomb repulsion) in mesoscopic interference experiments.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to identify genuine quantum signatures arising from the interplay between fermionic exchange symmetry and Coulomb repulsion in two-electron interference experiments.

**Main result.** The theory predicts characteristic features, including a pronounced Coulomb-dominated suppression region and phase offsets, which are absent in semiclassical descriptions. The spatial oscillation frequency outside this region is governed by fermionic exchange symmetry.

**Method.** The analysis employs a fully quantum-mechanical framework, calculating the second-order spatial correlation function using both wavefunction and field operator approaches.

**Model / system.** The system models two electrons emitted from two independent needle-tip emitters in free space, treated as a two-body scattering problem governed by a Hamiltonian including kinetic energy and Coulomb interaction. The wave function is separated into center-of-mass and relative coordinates.

**Key observables.** The second-order spatial correlation function, G^(2)(r1, r2), which measures the probability of detecting two electrons at specific positions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Tip separation ($r_0$), detector distance ($D$), electron energy ($\kappa$ or $k$), and bias voltage ($U_{	ext{bias}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes point-like sources, monochromatic emission, and instantaneous acceleration to the detectors in the far field.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the experimental setup with two needle tips. Figure 2 depicts the quantum paths corresponding to parallel and crossed paths for the relative wavefunction.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first establishing the general framework using a fully quantum-mechanical description, then deriving the correlation function via both wavefunction and field operator methods, and finally analyzing the distinct signatures arising from Coulomb interaction versus exchange symmetry.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding how fermionic exchange and Coulomb repulsion jointly shape two-electron correlations is essential for identifying genuine quantum signatures in multi-electron interference experiments. To address this interplay, we investigate Hanbury Brown and Twiss interference of two electrons generated by two independent needle-tip emitters within a full quantum-mechanical framework. In the absence of Coulomb interaction, the approach reproduces the results previously obtained within a quantum path formalism. For Coulomb-interacting electrons, we predict characteristic features absent in a semiclassical description: a pronounced Coulomb-dominated suppression region as well as Coulomb-induced phase offsets and fringe shifts. At the same time, outside of the Coulomb-dominated region, the spatial oscillation frequency is essentially governed by fermionic exchange symmetry. Our results establish quantitative parameter regimes for disentangling Coulomb interaction from fermionic exchange symmetry in such experiments.

</details>

### [A Simple Algorithm for Best Separable State](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10147v1)

**Authors:** Prashanti Anderson, Samuel B. Hopkins, Amit Rajaraman  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.10147v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **3/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>
<div class="paper-figures-scroll">
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2608.10147_figures/2608.10147_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Figure 1: Two ways that ∥Cov ∥F can decrease as we condition on coordinates in a high- dimensional distribution. In the top row, correlations disappear while the conditional mean remains near the origin – the distribution becomes isotropic but retains significant variance. In the bottom row, the overall variance decreases while the mean moves away from the origin. Our fine-grained pinning lemma shows that the second behavior must happen for a noticeable fraction of conditional distributions.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper presents a new, simpler algorithm to solve the Best Separable State (BSS) problem, which quantifies how well a quantum measurement can distinguish between entangled and separable states. By improving upon existing Sum-of-Squares relaxation techniques, the authors achieve significantly faster running times for finding near-optimal solutions in this fundamental problem of quantum resource theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** The techniques developed—advanced algebraic methods like refined pinning lemmas and efficient polynomial optimization solvers—are highly relevant for analyzing resource constraints in quantum information protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the Best Separable State (BSS) problem, which seeks to find the maximum acceptance probability of a quantum measurement restricted to unentangled (separable) states.

**Main result.** The authors develop an algorithm that significantly improves the time complexity for solving BSS in both the $\epsilon$-approximation regime ($1-\epsilon$) and the $q/n$ approximation regime, beating previous state-of-the-art bounds.

**Method.** The core method involves generalizing global correlation rounding techniques applied to Sum-of-Squares (SoS) relaxations of polynomial optimization problems over the unit sphere.

**Model / system.** The problem is formulated using a Hermitian matrix $M$ describing a quantum measurement device, studied in the context of finding optimal separable states $\langle x \otimes y, M(x \otimes y) angle$.

**Key observables.** BSS(M), approximation error ($\epsilon$), and the ratio $\|\Pi(x \otimes y)\|_2^2 / \|x \otimes y\|_2^2$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension $n$, the desired accuracy $\epsilon$, and the parameter $q$ defining the target separation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is simplified by working over $\mathbb{R}$, though the result generalizes from a symmetric case to the non-symmetric one. The proof relies on proving a new variant of the 'pinning lemma'.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces BSS, relates it to polynomial optimization and SoS relaxations, develops an improved rounding technique based on global correlation rounding, proves a novel pinning lemma, and applies this machinery to achieve the final time complexity bounds for distinguishing between different values of BSS(M).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the best separable state problem (BSS), which asks for the maximum acceptance probability of a quantum measurement over unentangled states. In classical terms, the goal is to maximize $\langle(x \otimes y), M (x \otimes y)\rangle$ over unit vectors $x,y$ where $0 \preceq M \preceq I$; we call this value $\mathrm{BSS}(M)$. We study $\mathrm{BSS}$ in the "perfect completeness" regime, where given $M$ such that $\mathrm{BSS}(M) = 1$ the goal is to find the best possible solution $x,y$ -- this generalizes the problem of finding a rank-one matrix as close as possible to a given subspace of $\mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ guaranteed to contain a rank-one matrix. The strongest known algorithmic guarantees for this problem are: (1) an algorithm which finds a solution with value $1-\varepsilon$ in time $\exp(\sqrt{n} (\log n)^{O(1)} / \varepsilon^2)$, due to Barak, Kothari, and Steurer, and (2) an algorithm which finds a solution with value $q/n$ in time roughly $n^{O(q)}$, due to Bhattiprolu, Ghosh, Guruswami, Lee, and Tulsiani. We give a much simpler approach to rounding the SoS relaxation, generalizing the canonical "global correlation rounding" technique, and obtain a better running time. Given $M$ with $\mathrm{BSS}(M) = 1$, our algorithm finds a solution with value $1-ε$ in time $n^{O(\sqrt{n/\varepsilon})}$, and a solution of value $q/n$ in time $n^{O(\sqrt q)}$. Using the same techniques, we prove a new variant of the "pinning lemma", a measure-decomposition theorem widely used in LP/SDP rounding, high-dimensional probability, and statistical physics, which we believe is of independent interest.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses inelastic neutron scattering on $	ext{CrBr}_2$ and $	ext{CrI}_2$ to determine that their helimagnetic order stems from the competition between intrachain and interchain antiferromagnetic interactions. The study quantifies this by fitting spin wave data, finding that single-ion anisotropy modulates the helix, while also revealing evidence of structural disorder in the crystal stacking.

**Why it may be interesting.** The detailed analysis of spin wave dispersions and the extraction of multiple competing exchange couplings provide a quantitative understanding of magnetic frustration in low-dimensional quantum magnets, which is highly relevant for designing novel quantum materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to determine the unknown magnetic interactions responsible for the helimagnetic order observed in the layered compounds $	ext{CrBr}_2$ and $	ext{CrI}_2$, while also investigating how dimensionality affects this ordering.

**Main result.** Helimagnetism primarily arises from the competition between nearest-neighbor antiferromagnetic exchange interactions within chains ($	ext{J}_1$) and between chains ($	ext{J}'_1$). Furthermore, single-ion anisotropy significantly modulates the helical spin structure.

**Method.** The research heavily relies on inelastic neutron scattering (INS) measurements on single crystals to map out spin wave dispersions and extract magnetic exchange constants. Elastic neutron scattering is also used to probe structural disorder.

**Model / system.** The physical system consists of van der Waals layered magnets, $	ext{CrBr}_2$ and $	ext{CrI}_2$, which exhibit quasi-2D magnetism. The analysis uses spin wave theory applied to Heisenberg Hamiltonians incorporating competing exchange couplings ($J_1, J'_1$) and single-ion anisotropy (SIA).

**Key observables.** Spin wave dispersion ($\omega(Q)$), magnetic Bragg peak intensities, incommensurability ($\delta$), and the temperature dependence of spin correlations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dominant exchange interactions are $	ext{J}_1$ and $	ext{J}'_1$; SIA values are significant (e.g., $-0.16 	ext{ meV}$ for $	ext{CrBr}_2$); interlayer coupling ($J_c$) is found to be weak.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the observed magnetic behavior can be modeled by nearest-neighbor exchange interactions and incorporates corrections from single-ion anisotropy and higher-order harmonic terms in INS fitting.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate lattice structures, elastic intensity measurements showing disordered stacking, and INS data revealing spin wave dispersions with avoided crossings attributed to SIA and $3k_M$ harmonics.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the magnetic order via INS, then fitting exchange parameters using simplified models ($J_1-J'_1$), refining the model by incorporating SIA effects visible in avoided crossings, and finally analyzing structural disorder and higher-order harmonic peaks to constrain the full Hamiltonian.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

CrBr$_2$ and CrI$_2$ are promising platforms for studying the effect of dimensionality on magnetic order, having been isolated in a 3-, 2-, and 1-dimensional form as bulk crystals, monolayers, and individual chains encapsulated in carbon nanotubes. However, the interactions that give rise to their helimagnetic order are unknown. Via inelastic neutron scattering on single crystals, we have determined the exchange interactions of these compounds from the spin wave dispersions, finding that the helimagnetic order arises primarily from competing antiferromagnetic interactions between intrachain and interchain nearest-neighbors. Single-ion anisotropy is substantial, modulating the helical spin rotation and gapping the inelastic intensity at points of branch crossings. As temperature increases, long-range order vanishes but intralayer correlations remain detectable up to at least 50 K.

</details>

**Authors:** Hang Xu, Tailong Xiao, Ze Zheng, Xiaoyang Deng, Jinfeng Zheng, Jingzheng Huang, Guihua Zeng  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.10490v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **3/5** · `interference shaping light` **1/5**

</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper addresses the fundamental challenge of performing high-precision measurements involving multiple unknown parameters simultaneously. By introducing the concept of Indefinite Evolution (IE), the authors demonstrate a novel resource that allows quantum systems to overcome inherent encoding and measurement incompatibilities. This breakthrough opens a pathway toward realizing true Heisenberg-limited sensing in complex, multi-parameter physical platforms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete theoretical mechanism (IE) for achieving optimal sensing bounds in complex multi-parameter settings, which is highly relevant for designing next-generation quantum sensors based on interferometry or atomic clocks.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Extending quantum metrology precision from single-parameter estimation (Heisenberg limit) to multiparameter estimation is fundamentally constrained by parameter-encoding and measurement incompatibility.

**Main result.** The introduction of Indefinite Evolution (IE) overcomes these incompatibilities, enabling the simultaneous achievement of Heisenberg-limited scaling for multiple parameters in quantum sensing.

**Method.** The authors propose a framework utilizing IE, where control operations are placed in a coherent superposition to allow compatible parameter encoding and optimal measurement strategies.

**Model / system.** The model involves quantum probing using unitary evolution $U_h = e^{-iH(h)t}$ with unknown parameters $h=(h_1, \dots, h_n)$ encoded by noncommuting generators. The analysis is generalized to noisy, many-body, and high-dimensional probes.

**Key observables.** Quantum Fisher Information Matrix (QFIM), Classical Fisher Information Matrix (CFIM), Simultaneous Heisenberg Limit (SHL) scaling ($F \propto t^2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The relationship between signal generators (orthogonal vs. parallel); the presence or absence of Signal Reversal (SR).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that IE acts as an operational resource capable of circumventing fundamental limitations imposed by definite evolution protocols.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 compares precision estimates for different strategies (FE, QEC, IE) showing IE achieves SHL where others fail; other figures illustrate the general protocol structure and scaling in multi-qubit/high-dimensional settings.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the limitations of standard metrology protocols due to encoding/measurement incompatibility. It then introduces and details the IE framework, demonstrating its success across various generator types (orthogonal, non-parallel, parallel) and generalizing the results to noisy and high-dimensional systems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum metrology achieves Heisenberg-limited precision in single-parameter estimation, but its multiparameter extension is fundamentally constrained by both parameter-encoding and measurement incompatibility. Noncommuting signal generators may cause incompatible parameter-encoding, preventing the quantum Fisher information matrix from simultaneously achieving the Heisenberg scale for all parameters. Due to incompatible optimal measurements, the classical Fisher information matrix represents the practical attainable precision. Here, we introduce a multiparameter metrology framework based on indefinite evolution (IE), in which different control operations and signal reversal are placed in a coherent superposition. For a single-qubit probe with mutually orthogonal signal generators, IE enables compatible parameter encoding and optimal measurement without the signal reversal. For parallel generators, where only signal reversal realized by its generator is available, IE can achieve the same performance. We further extend this mechanism to noisy, many-body, and high-dimensional probes, and establish general conditions for achieving the simultaneous Heisenberg-limit. In contrast, definite evolution cannot achieve the same performance under compatible optimal measurements, even when signal reversal is available. Our results identify IE as an operational resource for overcoming multiparameter incompatibility and open a route toward attainable Heisenberg-limited sensing in interferometric platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper constructs a detailed quantum circuit realization for the attention mechanism used in Transformers, specifically when inputs are probability distributions. It shows that core components like softmax and temperature can be mapped exactly to sequences of quantum measurements and rotations. This work bridges advanced deep learning architectures with fundamental principles of quantum information processing.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on AI, the rigorous mapping of continuous mathematical functions (like softmax) to discrete quantum operations (Born measurements, rotations) provides deep insights into how complex classical computations can be fundamentally constrained or realized within quantum information theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop an exact, component-by-component quantum realization for the softmax attention mechanism, particularly when inputs are constrained to the probability simplex.

**Main result.** It establishes that softmax attention admits such a quantum analog in the infinite-shot limit, mapping classical components like temperature and residual connections to specific quantum gates and measurement protocols.

**Method.** The realization uses quantum primitives including amplitude encoding, block-encoded projections, Hadamard tests for inner products, and controlled rotations ($	ext{c}R_y$) within a circuit framework.

**Model / system.** The model is the single-head gated transformer attention layer operating on inputs restricted to the probability simplex. The computation is mapped onto a quantum circuit utilizing qubits and unitary transformations.

**Key observables.** Attention scores, which are realized as Hadamard-test statistics on block-encoded projections of amplitude-encoded inputs.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The softmax temperature ($eta$) is physically realized by the number ($L$) of post-selected measurement rounds; learnable parameters correspond to rotation-gate angles.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The equivalence is exact only in the infinite-shot limit. The construction assumes inputs are row-normalized probability distributions (the probability simplex domain).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 provides a 'dictionary at a glance' mapping classical components to quantum circuits; Figure 2 illustrates Born-rule encoding, and Figure 3 shows Q/K projection using block encoding.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds an equivalence dictionary by realizing core attention components (scores, softmax, temperature, residual) sequentially. It details the necessary quantum primitives for each classical function, culminating in a full layer equivalence theorem machine-checked in Lean 4.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The attention mechanism forms the foundation of many modern AI models such as the Transformer. In one subclass of problems where attention is used, inputs and outputs are bound to the probability simplex so that all outputs sum to one. In this setting, softmax attention admits an exact, component-by-component quantum realization. Attention scores are Hadamard-test statistics on block-encoded projections of amplitude-encoded inputs. The exponential softmax is the interior of a cosine-squared family generated by Born-rule measurement under an exact bijection, whose boundary expresses sparse attention with exact zeros at finite parameter values. The softmax temperature is a repetition count where post-selected measurement rounds realize discretized inverse temperature exactly. Value aggregation is a deterministic column-loading channel that dilates the column-stochastic value matrix. The gated residual is the preparation angle of a single ancilla, with the additive identity at a mixing angle of π/2. Every learnable parameter is a rotation-gate angle. The composed layer is exact in the infinite-shot limit with one measure-and-reload step per attention score; a fully-coherent variant is ε-approximate via quantum singular value transformation in the infinite depth limit. The algebraic core is machine-checked in Lean 4.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theoretical framework to enhance Bell-pair self-testing using finite-energy GKP states. By incorporating measurements based on the photon number modulo four, the authors demonstrate that the achievable CHSH violation and extractability bounds exceed previous limits. This work is significant for quantum optics as it provides a pathway toward device-independent verification of entanglement in realistic physical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete, resource-efficient method for achieving device-independent quantum guarantees (Bell testing) in continuous variable systems by engineering measurement bases using discrete information (photon number modulo 4).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to establish robust self-testing protocols for Bell pairs using finite-energy Gaussian states (GKP) in continuous variable systems.

**Main result.** The analysis provides quantitative thresholds showing that the CHSH value and extractability bound can be significantly improved by incorporating deterministic phase-bit coarse-graining derived from modular photon-number information.

**Method.** The authors employ a full-oscillator analysis, combining measurements on position/momentum with fixed binary coarse-grainings of photon number modulo four to calculate physical correlations without logical post-corrections.

**Model / system.** The system is based on bosonic modes encoded using the Gottesman–Kitaev–Preskill (GKP) code. The analysis focuses on finite-energy filtered versions of these states, allowing for measurements that utilize modular photon-number information.

**Key observables.** CHSH value ($S$), extractability bound ($\Xi(ho 	o |\psi_Bangle)$), and model thresholds (e.g., 4.56 dB).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The canonical displacement amplitude $d$, the filter parameter $eta$, and the finite-energy cutoff/approximation level ($n$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results are 'honest-model predictions' that do not account for experimental imperfections like loss or detection efficiency, though a device-independent guarantee is discussed.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by analyzing the extractability bound using finite-rank projections and then applies this to the GKP state structure, showing how modular photon-number measurements enhance the self-testing capability beyond standard Pauli settings.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a full-oscillator analysis of a finite-energy GKP CHSH test whose observed score yields robust Bell-pair self-testing. Periodically binned position and momentum give the Pauli settings, while a fixed binary coarse-graining of photon number modulo four and its displaced conjugate realize the tilted settings. For a number-filtered GKP source, we retain the finite codeword overlap, define the measurements on all photon-number sectors, and compute the physical correlations without logical post-corrections. With the canonical ideal-logical displacement \(d=\sqrtπ\), the CHSH value exceeds the local bound above \(4.56\) dB of per-peak squeezing, and Kaniewski's extractability bound becomes nontrivial above \(5.02\) dB. Calibrating only \(d\) using an independently characterized finite-energy parameter lowers these model thresholds to \(4.21\) dB and \(4.58\) dB, respectively; at \(12\) dB, it raises the score from \(2.69486\) to \(2.78858\) and the corresponding target-state overlap bound from \(0.90758\) to \(0.97243\). This calibration is fixed before Bell-test data are collected. The displacement activates the odd modulo-four sectors, so their fixed a priori assignments are a genuine finite-energy component. The large gain is specific to the deterministic phase-bit coarse-graining; independently calibrating the one-bit POVM with randomized odd-sector outcomes gives only a much smaller improvement. These are honest-model predictions, not loss, detection-efficiency, or finite-sample thresholds. In an experiment, a device-independent guarantee for an extracted Bell pair follows by inserting a confidence lower bound on the observed CHSH score into the self-testing theorem.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper re-examines Erwin Schrödinger's views on determinism, arguing that he was not a resistor of quantum indeterminism. Instead, it details his early arguments for fundamental randomness based on statistical mechanics and the nature of physical laws. It is important for understanding the philosophical foundations of quantum theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides crucial historical context for understanding the philosophical underpinnings of quantum mechanics, which informs modern discussions on measurement problems and foundations of physics relevant to open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the historical misconception that Schrödinger resisted quantum indeterminism, focusing on his actual views regarding determinism versus inherent randomness in physics.

**Main result.** Schrödinger's perspective was fundamentally indeterministic, arguing that physical laws are inherently statistical and challenging the necessity of deterministic descriptions even at classical levels.

**Method.** The analysis involves historical interpretation of Schrödinger's writings, particularly his 1931 essay on indeterminism, to reconstruct his philosophical stance.

**Model / system.** The discussion centers on quantum mechanics itself, specifically contrasting the deterministic evolution implied by the wave function with the probabilistic nature observed in measurements and statistical laws.

**Key observables.** Statistical outcomes (as opposed to single deterministic outcomes), irreversibility, and information content.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that a detailed reading of Schrödinger's work reveals a more radical indeterministic stance than commonly portrayed.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first establishing the common portrayal of Schrödinger as anti-indeterminism, then systematically presenting his arguments from 1931 demonstrating an early embrace of fundamental statistical and indeterminate principles.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Erwin Schrödinger is often portrayed as a reactionary who resisted the indeterminism introduced by quantum mechanics, but on closer inspection his views on determinism prove to be more complex and more radical.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proves that a simple setup—a single atom placed near a mirror—can act as a Universal Reservoir Computer. By analyzing the system's dynamics in a linearized limit, the authors provide explicit constructive proofs for its universality. This work is significant because it demonstrates machine learning capability using minimal, passive quantum hardware components.

**Why it may be interesting.** It directly applies advanced concepts from open quantum systems (Lindblad dynamics, cavity QED) to solve a problem in machine learning (Reservoir Computing), showing how fundamental physical constraints can yield universal computation capabilities.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To demonstrate that a single, minimal physical quantum device can function as a universal reservoir computer, overcoming limitations associated with requiring entire classes of reservoirs.

**Main result.** The authors prove that a single atom placed in front of a mirror is a provably universal reservoir computer under specific constructive conditions. This establishes universality using only one passive quantum object.

**Method.** The proof utilizes the linear-transducer limit approximation, constructing explicit mathematical recipes based on geometric parameters and measurement settings to achieve arbitrary accuracy for fading-memory map approximation.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a single two-level emitter (atom) interacting with its electromagnetic field modes reflected by a mirror. The dynamics are governed by a Lindblad master equation, incorporating memory effects from delayed light interaction and nonlinearity from atomic saturation.

**Key observables.** Test NRMSE for dephasing/jitter; approximation error bounds ($\delta_{MN}N + A(M, N) \sqrt{2/M} ar{r}^M + e ar{\epsilon}$); feedback loss as a function of retained power fraction $\eta$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Atom–mirror distance $L$; number of accessible field modes ($K$); linearization parameter $s$; memory depth ($M$) and mode number ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core proof is established in the linear-transducer limit; achieving universality requires satisfying four constructive geometric/measurement conditions (D1–D6).

**Figures summary.** Figures demonstrate error decay vs. accessible modes, quantify nonlinearity transfer by comparing readout types on target functions, and show feedback loss recovery upon restoring a small fraction of the coupling.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model via master equations, derives the linear-transducer limit for theoretical proof (Theorem 1), provides constructive conditions for universality, and concludes with comparisons to other reservoir architectures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Universal approximation in reservoir computing is typically associated with a class of reservoirs. We show that universality can be associated with a single reservoir, considering a minimal setup of a single atom in front of a mirror. In its linear-transducer limit, our reservoir is a universal approximator of fading-memory maps under an operating class of checkable conditions, with a rate constant measured at the operating point. A given reservoir can reach arbitrary accuracy by changing measurement settings. The proof gives an explicit recipe: for a target accuracy, it specifies the required physical resources and resonator modes. Enlarging the number of accessible modes increases the matchable kernel span without reducing capability. Beyond the linear limit, the atom's saturation replaces high-order polynomial readouts, and the device operates on real-world tasks alongside classical baselines. Our results highlight an example of universality with a minimal quantum setup.

</details>

**Authors:** Xingkun Song, Huiqiu Lin

**Summary.** This paper develops rigorous mathematical criteria to determine when a quantum state transfer between two nodes on a mixed graph is impossible (zero transfer). It achieves this by analyzing the spectral properties of the graph's Hermitian adjacency matrix, providing specific nonexistence results for prime-ordered circulant graphs and detailed classifications for composite orders.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides deep mathematical tools for understanding quantum transport phenomena, which are foundational to designing robust quantum communication channels or characterizing decoherence mechanisms in complex quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper characterizes the phenomenon of 'zero transfer'—where quantum state transition amplitudes vanish for all time—on mixed graphs.

**Main result.** Zero transfer is characterized by vanishing entries in the spectral idempotents associated with the graph's Hermitian adjacency matrix. For oriented circulant graphs of prime order, zero transfer cannot occur.

**Method.** The analysis uses advanced linear algebra techniques, including Taylor series expansions and spectral decomposition theorems applied to quantum walks governed by a Hermitian Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The model is a continuous-time quantum walk on a mixed graph $\Gamma$, whose evolution is dictated by the Hermitian adjacency matrix $H_\Gamma$. The system involves both undirected edges and directed arcs.

**Key observables.** Zero transfer (vanishing transition amplitude $U(t)_{u,v} = 0$ for all $t \ge 0$), spectral idempotent entries $(E_{	heta_r})_{u,v}$, and the structure of the graph's adjacency matrix.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Graph order ($n$), primality of $n$, and the specific connectivity defined by the generating set $C$ in circulant graphs.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis heavily relies on the properties of Hermitian matrices, spectral decomposition, and algebraic number theory related to roots of unity when analyzing circulant structures.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 provides an example of zero transfer for a specific mixed graph $G(\mathbb{Z}_{12}, \{2, 3, 8\})$, showing the set of vertices exhibiting this property. Tables summarize counts and sets for various small even orders ($n \le 20$).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from general necessary conditions (Taylor expansion) to more precise spectral characterizations (spectral idempotents), culminating in detailed case studies on oriented circulant graphs, especially analyzing the prime vs. composite order cases.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this paper, we investigate zero transfer on mixed graphs. Zero transfer is a quantum walk phenomenon in which the transition amplitude between two vertices is identically zero for all times, so that no quantum state transfer occurs between them. Using the Hermitian adjacency matrix, we derive necessary and sufficient conditions for zero transfer in mixed graphs. We then specialize these criteria to oriented circulant graphs, obtaining nonexistence results for prime order, structural restrictions for even order, and exhaustive computational classifications for small orders.

</details>

**Summary.** This work extends the Master Stability Function theory to analyze synchronization in complex, weighted, directed networks with time delays. It demonstrates that network heterogeneity and nonreciprocity are crucial resources for maintaining stable synchronization. The paper provides optimization techniques to design optimal network topologies that enhance collective stability.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical coupled oscillators, the mathematical machinery—analyzing spectral properties of large matrices derived from complex dynamics ($\dot{\eta} = J_1 \eta + J_2 \eta(t-	au)$) and optimizing structure for stability—is highly relevant to understanding collective behavior in quantum systems like superconducting circuits or photonic arrays.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper extends synchronization analysis, using the Master Stability Function (MSF) framework, to complex delay-coupled networks that exhibit degree heterogeneity, weighting, and directionality.

**Main result.** Synchronization stability in these heterogeneous, delayed networks can be enhanced by structural nonreciprocity and network heterogeneity, potentially surpassing the stability achieved in simpler all-to-all or non-delayed systems.

**Method.** The authors generalize the MSF framework by analyzing the system's dynamics using Jordan canonical form analysis to handle time delays and complex coupling structures. They develop an optimization method to find optimal edge weights maximizing synchronization stability.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes delay-coupled networks of identical oscillators, exemplified by the Stuart-Landau (SL) and Lang-Kobayashi (LK) models. The dynamics are governed by coupled differential equations incorporating time delays ($	au$).

**Key observables.** Synchronization stability, quantified by the maximum transverse Lyapunov exponent (MTLE), $\lambda_{\max}$ (must be negative for stability); Stability region ($\Omega$) in the complex plane.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time delay ($	au$), coupling strength ($\kappa$), network structure parameters (degree distribution, weights $A_{jk}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The standard MSF assumptions are violated by time delays; the method addresses this using Jordan canonical form analysis. Optimization relies on iterative perturbation methods.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how increasing delay ($	au$) shrinks the stability region $\Omega$ for SL models, and show optimization procedures driving networks towards heterogeneous, directional structures that maximize synchronizability.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the generalization of MSF to handle delays and heterogeneity. It then applies this framework to specific models (SL/LK), demonstrating how structural properties like indegree determine stability boundaries. Finally, it presents network optimization and sparsification analyses to quantify robustness.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Time delays are ubiquitous in physical and biological networked systems, playing a fundamental role in the emergence and stability of collective behavior. Yet, existing theoretical methods for synchronization analysis of delay-coupled systems are largely limited to networks with homogeneous degree distributions. Here, we extend the master stability function framework to a broad class of degree-heterogeneous, weighted, and directed delay-coupled networks. The analysis reveals that synchronization can be enhanced in heterogeneous networks compared to homogeneous ones, including all-to-all networks, which are known to be optimal in non-delayed systems. To identify optimal heterogeneous structures, we develop a network optimization method that finds directional, edge-weighted configurations maximizing synchronization stability. Our results show that, in delay-coupled networks, heterogeneity and nonreciprocity are key resources for synchronization.

</details>

### [Inferring stealthy hyperuniform correlations from quantum transport](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11188v1)

**Summary.** The paper introduces a novel technique to determine the stealthiness parameter ($\chi$) characterizing hyperuniform disorder by analyzing quantum transport measurements. Using a 1D tight-binding model, they show that the energy-dependent transmittance spectrum acts as a unique fingerprint of this correlation. This allows researchers to extract crucial structural information from readily measurable electrical conductance data.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful example of using transport spectroscopy as an inverse diagnostic tool. For AMO/open quantum systems, this methodology could be adapted to characterize complex disorder potentials (e.g., engineered lattice impurities or random coupling strengths) in mesoscopic devices where direct structural characterization is intractable.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is developing a method to infer the structural properties, specifically 'stealthy hyperuniform correlations' characterized by parameter $\chi$, from measurable quantum transport data.

**Main result.** Transmittance spectra serve as unique fingerprints of stealthy hyperuniform disorder, allowing for the recovery of the correlation parameter $\chi$ via an inverse protocol.

**Method.** The authors propose and implement a conductance-based inverse protocol by minimizing a misfit function between calculated energy-dependent transmittance $T(E)$ and a target spectrum.

**Model / system.** The model is a one-dimensional tight-binding chain subjected to on-site disorder. This disorder is specifically engineered in momentum space to exhibit stealthy hyperuniform correlations, defined by a spectral cutoff $S(k) = \Theta(|k| - K)$, where $K=2\pi\chi$.

**Key observables.** Energy-dependent transmittance $T(E)$ (or conductance), and the misfit function value.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Stealthiness parameter $\chi$, disorder strength $W$, and the energy window $[E_-, E_+]$ used for fitting.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the transport response function $T(E)$ retains a unique signature of the underlying structural correlations ($\chi$) even when direct measurement of the disorder potential is impossible.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the real-space potential for varying $\chi$, show how increasing $W$ suppresses $T(E)$ while keeping the spectral drop position stable, and demonstrate the systematic shift of the transparent energy window as $\chi$ changes.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem (inferring $\chi$), details the model setup (1D chain with stealthy disorder), outlines the computational method (RGF for $T(E)$), and culminates in demonstrating the inverse protocol by minimizing a misfit function over the energy spectrum to recover $(\chi, W)$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Stealthy hyperuniform disordered systems exhibit strongly suppressed long-wavelength fluctuations, producing correlated disorder with unusual consequences for wave propagation. A central quantity characterizing these systems is the stealthiness parameter $χ$, which controls the range of excluded Fourier components in the disorder spectrum. However, in realistic settings, the microscopic disorder configuration may not be directly accessible, making it challenging to determine $χ$ from structural information alone. Here, we propose a conductance-based inverse protocol to recover stealthy hyperuniform correlations from transport data. As a proof of concept, we study spinless fermions in a one-dimensional tight-binding chain connected to clean semi-infinite leads, with on-site disorder generated by imposing a stealthy spectrum $S(k)=Θ(|k|-K)$, where $K=2πχ$. The energy-dependent transmittance is computed using a recursive Green's function method and compared with target spectra through a misfit function defined over an energy window. We show that the position of the sharp drop separating high- and low-transmittance regions is strongly controlled by $χ$, while the disorder strength $W$ mainly affects the absolute magnitude of the transmittance. As a result, the misfit function displays a clear minimum close to the target stealthy parameter. Our results demonstrate that transmittance spectra can serve as fingerprints of stealthy hyperuniform disorder, providing a practical route to infer correlated-disorder parameters from transport measurements.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores the competition between disorder (Anderson localization) and non-reciprocity (NHSE) in a two-leg ladder model. By tuning the inter-chain coupling asymmetry, the authors demonstrate successive transitions in eigenstate behavior, showing that robust skin localization can persist even when the energy spectrum topology changes away from conventional point gaps.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical framework for understanding how competing non-Hermitian and random potentials dictate fundamental transport properties like localization, which has implications for designing robust quantum devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the complex interplay between disorder-induced Anderson localization and non-Hermitian effects, specifically the Non-Hermitian Skin Effect (NHSE).

**Main result.** Tuning the asymmetry of inter-chain coupling drives successive transitions in eigenstate nature: from skin localization to Anderson localization, and back to skin localization even when the energy spectrum exhibits a line-gap topology.

**Method.** The analysis involves calculating the Inverse Participation Ratio (IPR) and Mean Center of Mass ($	ext{mcom}$) averaged over disorder realizations to diagnose the spatial extent and localization type of eigenstates.

**Model / system.** The model is a two-leg ladder system comprising a Hatano–Nelson chain coupled to a Hermitian chain via asymmetric inter-chain hopping. The Hamiltonian incorporates onsite disorder terms ($\Delta_j$) and coupling parameters ($t, a$).

**Key observables.** Inverse Participation Ratio ($	ext{IPR}$), Mean Center of Mass ($\langle 	ext{mcom} angle$), and the complex energy spectrum topology (point-gap vs. line-gap).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The asymmetry parameter '$a$' is the primary control, which tunes the competition between localization mechanisms; critical points include $a=0$, $a=t$, and $a>t$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on treating the system as a tight-binding model with disorder, focusing on the spectral and spatial properties of eigenstates.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the transition from boundary localization (skin effect) to delocalized states at $a=t$, confirming these transitions via $\langle 	ext{IPR} angle$ minima and $\langle 	ext{mcom} angle$ peaks at the critical coupling strength.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes how varying the inter-chain asymmetry parameter '$a$' drives successive phase transitions in localization, mapping out regimes where NHSE dominates versus where disorder-induced Anderson localization prevails.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The interplay between disorder and non-Hermitian effects gives rise to a variety of intriguing localization phenomena. While disorder tends to localize the eigenstates through Anderson localization, non-Hermitian non-reciprocity promotes the formation of skin modes by driving the eigenstates toward the system boundaries giving rise to non-Hermitian skin effect (NHSE). In this work, we investigate the interplay between these competing mechanisms in a two-leg ladder consisting of a Hatano-Nelson chain coupled to a Hermitian chain via asymmetric inter-chain hopping, with strong disorder present in both chains. We show that tuning the asymmetry of the inter-chain coupling induces successive transitions in the nature of the eigenstates, from skin localization to Anderson localization and subsequently back to skin localization. Remarkably, the non-Hermitian skin effect re-emerges even though the energy spectrum exhibits a line-gap topology, demonstrating that robust skin localization can persist beyond the conventional point-gap regime.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides a rigorous theoretical analysis for using optomechanical systems to search for signatures of quantum gravity, specifically testing the Generalized Uncertainty Principle. It critically reevaluates previous models by incorporating higher-order nonlinear dynamics and realistic laser phase noise into both Fabry-Perot and membrane-in-the-middle setups. The key finding is that these neglected terms significantly degrade predicted measurement sensitivities, demanding more careful experimental design.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it pushes the boundaries of quantum sensing by rigorously accounting for non-ideal experimental factors (higher-order couplings and noise) that often invalidate simplified theoretical predictions in advanced AMO experiments.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is developing accurate theoretical methods to detect minute quantum gravity effects, such as those predicted by the Generalized Uncertainty Principle (GUP), using highly sensitive optomechanical systems.

**Main result.** Previous idealized estimates for achievable resolution are significantly overestimated; incorporating higher-order nonlinear couplings and realistic laser phase noise is crucial for any practical assessment of these quantum gravity tests.

**Method.** The analysis involves reanalyzing the system dynamics by incorporating full nonlinear Hamiltonian terms and modeling environmental decoherence via Langevin equations, moving beyond simple linear approximations.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on two experimentally relevant platforms: a Fabry-Perot (FP) cavity optomechanical system and a membrane-in-the-middle (MiM) system. The model incorporates GUP corrections into the mechanical oscillator Hamiltonian, leading to nonlinear terms in the dynamics.

**Key observables.** The effective oscillation frequency shift ($\omega_{	ext{eff}}$), which is used as a quantitative diagnostic signal to determine the dimensionless GUP parameter ($eta_{NL}$), measured via phase-modulated probe light and homodyne detection.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensionless GUP parameter ($eta_{NL}$); coupling strengths ($g_{F0}, g_{M0}$); and noise characteristics, including laser phase noise spectrum $S_\Phi(\omega_p)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis uses the c-number stochastic Langevin equation approximation in some regimes, which neglects certain nonlinear quantum effects for mathematical tractability.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the optomechanical detector setup, detailing components like EOMs and balanced homodyne detection, and showing the configuration options (FP vs. MiM).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework by modifying the Hamiltonian to include GUP corrections; it then details the dynamics for both FP and MiM systems using Langevin equations; finally, it derives revised measurement protocols and sensitivity bounds.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Optomechanical systems offer a promising platform for observing dynamical signatures of quantum gravity through precision measurements of quantum harmonic oscillator dynamics. However, most existing analyses consider only the linear radiation-pressure interaction while neglecting higher-order optomechanical couplings and laser phase noise. These neglected contributions can be comparable in magnitude to the predicted quantum-gravity corrections and may therefore introduce spurious signals or mask the genuine physical effect. Here we reanalyze two experimentally realized platforms, a Fabry-Perot optomechanical system and a membrane-in-the-middle optomechanical system, by incorporating the complete nonlinear dynamics and realistic laser phase noise. Using measured device parameters, we derive revised protocols for generalized uncertainty principle tests and establish practical sensitivity bounds. Our results demonstrate that previous idealized estimates significantly overestimate the achievable resolution, underscoring the necessity of including higher-order interactions and implementing effective laser phase noise suppression in realistic assessments of optomechanical quantum gravity tests.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically derives the microscopic origin of piezomagnetism in higher-spin altermagnets by analyzing how lattice distortions couple to magnetic excitations. The key finding is a stark contrast between integer and half-integer spins: integer spins exhibit enhanced response due to a specific excitation mode softening into a Higgs-like amplitude mode, while this mechanism fails for half-integer systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit link established between macroscopic magnetoelastic coupling (piezomagnetism) and the quantum dynamics/stability of higher-order magnetic excitations ($\mu=4n$ modes) provides a rich theoretical playground for understanding emergent properties in strongly correlated magnets, relevant to AMO systems exhibiting strong spin-orbit coupling.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the quantum mechanism by which lattice distortions (piezomagnetism) couple to and are mediated by higher-spin collective magnetic excitations in altermagnets.

**Main result.** Integer spin systems show a pronounced enhancement of piezomagnetism due to a specific multipolar excitation mode softening into a Higgs-like amplitude mode near the large-$D$ transition, contrasting with half-integer spins where this contribution is suppressed.

**Method.** The analysis employs flavor-wave theory and linear response theory applied to an effective Hamiltonian derived from mean-field decoupling of the spin system. This allows calculating susceptibilities based on excitation modes.

**Model / system.** The model is a two-sublattice antiferromagnet on a square lattice with higher spin $S$, governed by an exchange, second-neighbor interaction, and easy-plane anisotropy ($D$). The Hamiltonian is perturbed by small uniaxial lattice distortions ($\delta J, \delta K, \delta D$).

**Key observables.** Piezomagnetic susceptibility ($\chi_J, \chi_K, \chi_D$), uniform magnetization $\langle\hat{M}_{	ext{uni}}angle$, and the energy/mode content of quasiparticle excitation branches ($\omega_\mu(\mathbf{k})$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Spin quantum number $S$ (integer vs. half-integer), anisotropy strength ($D$), and lattice distortion coupling constants.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mean-field decoupling of the Hamiltonian, followed by bosonization/flavor-wave theory to describe excitations around the ground state.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how susceptibilities ($\chi_K, \chi_D$) vary with anisotropy $D/J$ for different spins, highlighting peaks near the large-$D$ transition for integer spins. Other figures detail the mode dependence of coupling matrix elements.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the theoretical framework by defining the Hamiltonian and using mean-field theory to establish excitation modes. It then applies linear response theory to calculate piezomagnetic susceptibilities, contrasting the behavior between integer and half-integer spin systems based on the fate of specific multipolar excitations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate piezomagnetism in higher-spin altermagnets with easy-plane single-ion anisotropy using a flavor-wave approach. We show that quantum fluctuations of higher-spin collective modes provide a microscopic origin of the piezomagnetic response. For integer spins, the relevant branch softens and evolves into a Higgs-like amplitude mode on approaching the large-$D$ transition, endowing the excitation with a sizable dipolar component and producing a pronounced enhancement of piezomagnetism. By contrast, in half-integer systems the higher-spin branches are progressively separated from the low-energy dipolar sector as the anisotropy increases, which suppresses their contribution to the response. This integer-half-integer contrast directly links macroscopic piezomagnetism to the low-energy fate of multipolar excitations. Our results establish piezomagnetism as a probe of higher-spin quantum dynamics and identify higher-spin altermagnets as a promising setting for quantum magnetoelastic responses.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a sophisticated theoretical method using Laguerre polynomials and sum rule constraints to analyze spin-singlet Fractional Quantum Hall fluids. By accurately determining the density-wave excitation gaps for both symmetric and antisymmetric modes, the authors provide crucial insights into the phase diagrams of bilayer FQH systems relevant to modern experimental platforms.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state physics, the rigorous mathematical techniques involving orthogonal polynomial expansions and constraint enforcement (sum rules) are highly relevant to developing robust theoretical tools for strongly interacting quantum many-body systems, which share conceptual links with AMO physics modeling of few-body or lattice excitations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To accurately calculate long-wavelength density-wave excitation gaps for spin-singlet Fractional Quantum Hall (FQH) fluids.

**Main result.** The authors show that incorporating sum rules derived from the static structure factor expansion is crucial for obtaining numerically stable and accurate values for both symmetric and antisymmetric density-wave excitation gaps.

**Method.** They perform thermodynamic fits on the pair correlation function using an orthogonal basis of associated Laguerre polynomials, enforcing necessary sum rules during the fitting procedure.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on two-component spin-singlet FQH fluids (Halperin and Jain states) in a strong perpendicular magnetic field. The framework is extended to analyze bilayer FQH systems.

**Key observables.** Long-wavelength Girvin-MacDonald-Platzman (GMP)/symmetric density-wave excitation gap, antisymmetric density-wave mode gap, and variational energies for phase diagram construction.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling fractions ($
u$), interlayer separation, and density imbalance/layer-polarization in bilayer systems.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes confinement to the Lowest Landau Level (LLL) and relies on expanding correlation functions using an orthogonal polynomial basis.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a theoretical framework by fitting pair correlation functions using Laguerre polynomials, enforcing sum rules derived from the static structure factor expansion. This is applied to calculate excitation gaps and ultimately construct phase diagrams for bilayer FQH systems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fractional quantum Hall (FQH) states are prototypical examples of strongly interacting topologically ordered systems. In this work, we obtain thermodynamic fits on the plane for the pair correlation function, and its Fourier transform, the static structure factor, of two-component spin-singlet Halperin and Jain FQH fluids by expanding them in the recently introduced basis of the orthogonal associated Laguerre polynomials [Fulsebakke et al., SciPost Phys. 14, 149 (2023), https://doi.org/10.21468/SciPostPhys.14.6.149 ] and ascertaining the expansion coefficients by fitting them to large-system Monte Carlo data evaluated using their trial wavefunctions. In this fitting procedure, aside from constraining the exact short-distance behavior of the wavefunction, we also derive and enforce the sum rules that the long-wavelength expansion of the static structure factor must adhere to. We show that incorporating these constraints is crucial for obtaining numerically stable and accurate values of the long-wavelength Girvin-MacDonald-Platzman (GMP)/symmetric density-wave excitation gap. We further extend this approach to spin-resolved density-correlation functions, enabling the evaluation of the gap of the antisymmetric density-wave mode for these spin-singlet FQH states. Finally, we use the density-correlators to compute variational energies of the states and construct phase diagrams for bilayer FQH systems. These could be relevant for understanding recent bilayer FQH experiments that map out the phase diagram by tuning the interlayer separation and density-imbalance/layer-polarization.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a systematic theory for quantifying the role of ancillary entanglement in quantum channel estimation. It introduces the concept of 'entanglement depth' ($k^*$), which determines the minimum required ancilla dimension to reach optimal measurement precision. The results provide concrete conditions under which entanglement offers no advantage, offering deep insights into resource limitations in quantum metrology.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous framework quantifying exactly how much entanglement resource is necessary to extract the maximum possible information about an unknown physical process (the channel).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates determining the minimum ancillary entanglement dimension ($k^*$) required to achieve optimal precision in quantum channel parameter estimation.

**Main result.** The optimal ancilla dimension $k^*$ is characterized by the minimum rank of a state that maximizes the achievable Fisher information, and sufficient conditions are provided for when no entanglement advantage exists ($k^*=1$).

**Method.** It uses variational characterization techniques to define the $k$-ancilla Fisher information, comparing it against the maximum possible value derived from optimizing over input states.

**Model / system.** The model involves estimating an unknown parameter $	heta$ characterizing a quantum channel $\{\Phi_	heta\}$ acting on a system Hilbert space $H$. The estimation process utilizes ancillary entanglement via an auxiliary system $H_k$.

**Key observables.** Symmetric Logarithmic Derivative (SLD) Fisher information ($J$), and the minimum required ancilla dimension ($k^*$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The channel parameter $	heta$, the input state rank $k$, and the dimensionality of the ancillary system.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes $\{\Phi_	heta\}$ is a smooth, piecewise-regular one-parameter family of quantum channels; specific results rely on assumptions like fixed-measurement Holevo representations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the relationship between achievable Fisher information ($J_k$) and the ancilla dimension $k$, aiming to find $k^*$ where $J_{k^*} = J_{	ext{max}}$.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the variational formula for $J_k$, derives theorems characterizing $k^*$ based on state rank, provides sufficient conditions (zero-gap) for $k^*=1$, and illustrates these results with explicit examples like depolarizing and amplitude damping channels.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the role of ancillary entanglement in quantum channel parameter estimation and investigate the minimal ancilla dimension required to achieve the maximum Fisher information. We introduce the $k$-ancilla Fisher information, which quantifies the optimal estimation precision achievable with input states of rank at most $k$, and derive a variational characterization in terms of a rank-constrained optimization problem. This formulation leads to a simple characterization of the minimum ancilla dimension $k^*$ required for optimal estimation, given by the minimum rank among the maximizers of the associated variational problem. We further identify sufficient conditions under which ancillary entanglement provides no advantage, including channel families admitting a fixed measure-and-prepare representation and channels satisfying a natural horizontality condition. In addition, we derive a bound on the incremental gain in Fisher information obtained by increasing the ancilla dimension under suitable structural assumptions on the optimal input states. The general results are illustrated through explicit examples, including unitary channels, qubit depolarizing and amplitude damping channels. These results provide a systematic framework for understanding and quantifying the entanglement resources required for optimal quantum channel estimation.

</details>
