**Summary.** This paper develops advanced techniques for constructing fault-tolerant quantum error correcting codes that support arbitrary Z-rotation logical gates. By extending the code switching protocol to new families of codes, such as those compatible with rotated surface codes, it significantly improves the theoretical feasibility and resource estimates for universal quantum computation beyond standard T-gate assumptions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly advances the theoretical understanding of resource efficiency in quantum computation by providing pathways to implement non-Clifford gates (like arbitrary Z-rotations) using more general and potentially less resource-intensive coding schemes than previously established methods.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is realizing a universal set of fault-tolerant quantum operations, specifically incorporating arbitrary Z-rotation logical gates (RZ(theta)), into existing quantum error correction frameworks.

**Main result.** The authors construct new classes of quantum color codes and $r$-orthogonal codes that support transversal RZ(theta) gates, extending the code switching protocol to these geometries.

**Method.** They utilize the doubling technique as a unified framework for constructing these codes and extend the code-switching method by incorporating fault-tolerant Z-rotation gate realization into protocols designed for rotated surface codes.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on quantum error correcting codes, including color codes and rotated surface codes. The goal is to encode logical qubits while maintaining high minimum distance ($d$) against noise.

**Key observables.** Minimum distance ($d$), qubit overhead (physical vs. logical), and the feasibility of fault-tolerant gate implementation (e.g., magic state preparation).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Arbitrarily large minimum distance, specific rotation angles $	ext{RZ}(	heta)$, and physical qubit counts (e.g., 45 qubits for a demonstration).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on extending protocols previously limited to T-gate supporting color codes; the construction methods assume certain weight divisibility conditions are met.

**Figures summary.** No specific figure summaries were extracted, but the notes mention circuit diagrams illustrating S|+> preparation via code-switching between different code types.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops theory by first establishing new color codes using a doubling technique to support RZ(theta) gates. It then generalizes this framework to $r$-orthogonal codes compatible with rotated surface codes, culminating in the extension of the fault-tolerant code switching protocol for these geometries.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

A technique for realizing a universal set of fault-tolerant quantum operations is the code switching method, which leverages two quantum codes with complementary sets of transversal gates. To date, the application of this technique has been largely limited to families of color codes supporting a logical $T$ gate. No analogous code switching protocols exist for many other prominent families, such as rotated surface codes, or for finer $Z$-rotation gates. In this work, we first utilize the doubling technique as a unified framework to construct a class of quantum color codes encoding a single logical qubit with an arbitrarily large minimum distance, enabling the transversal realization of arbitrary small logical $Z$-rotation gates. We investigate the structural properties of this code family, demonstrating that they improve upon the parameters of state-of-the-art triorthogonal codes, achieve lower qubit overhead compared to certain known color codes, and admit single-shot decoding of $Z$-syndromes via meta-checks. Furthermore, we show that this framework extends beyond color codes; specifically, it enables the generation of $r$-orthogonal quantum codes, $r \ge 2$, that inherit the local geometry of rotated surface codes. We then provide an overhead optimization protocol alongside several candidate codes tailored for realizing logical $Z$-rotation gates within rotated surface codes. Finally, we extend the fault-tolerant code switching protocol based on transversal CNOT gates to incorporate fault-tolerant realization of $Z$-rotation gates at any level of the Clifford hierarchy for geometries compatible with rotated surface codes. We present the first demonstration of fault-tolerant magic state preparation by means of code switching within a distance-three rotated surface code using a total footprint of only 45 physical qubits, and evaluate its performance through a simulation.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a formal framework to prove that certain advanced AI state-tracking tasks require fundamentally different amounts of memory and coordination resources depending on whether they are modeled classically or quantum mechanically. By establishing a 'semantic-compilation' theorem, the authors show provable separations—such as $\Omega(n^2)$ classical bits vs $n$ qubits for stabilizer contexts—highlighting genuine computational advantages beyond mere runtime speedups.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous theoretical tools (resource counting $B, M, D$) to quantify when quantum memory resources offer an advantage over classical computational structures, even when those structures are embedded within complex AI/NLP interfaces.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to rigorously prove inference-time quantum coordination advantages for specific AI state-tracking tasks by comparing resource costs between classical and quantum computation models.

**Main result.** It establishes a boundary-preserving semantic-compilation theorem, demonstrating separations where tracking noncommuting latent states requires super-polynomial classical memory ($B+M$) compared to the polynomial qubit requirement of a quantum model.

**Method.** The authors use resource accounting based on communication ($B$), persistent memory ($M$), and local work ($D$) to derive lower bounds for classical simulators, contrasting these with upper bounds derived from quantum state dimensions.

**Model / system.** The core models involve general generative processes simulated by both classical finite-state machines (characterized by $B$ and $M$ bits) and quantum latent-state generators, particularly focusing on stabilizer contexts ($\mathcal{P}_{	ext{stab}}^n$).

**Key observables.** Resource costs $(B, M, D)$; the separation between required classical coordination bits ($B+M$) and necessary qubits.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The size of the system $N$ or $n$; the approximation factor $\epsilon$; the complexity measures like $	ext{KD}\epsilon_{	ext{cl}}$ and $	ext{K}_{\epsilon}^{q}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The quantum advantage is restricted to tasks exhibiting contextuality (noncommuting measurements); the stabilizer result assumes exact simulation and ideal noiseless quantum memory; the analysis requires adaptive completeness.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining resource accounting for state-tracking, proving general lower bounds via a semantic-compilation theorem, applying this to specific QA/auditing tasks, and culminating in a strong separation proof using the stabilizer latent-state dialogue.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove inference-time quantum coordination advantages for specified AI state-tracking tasks. A solver compresses semantic history into a future-accessible boundary state and later answers a query. We count communication $B$, persistent instance-dependent memory $M$, and local work $D$; classical recurrence, caches, tools, and recomputation are allowed and charged. The central result is a boundary-preserving semantic-compilation theorem. It maps a finite one-way, streaming, or adaptive causal task into a semantic AI interface while preserving event order and access to past input. Classical boundary-state lower bounds and quantum-memory upper bounds transfer up to explicit compiler overhead, independently of the finite-precision recurrent architecture. Two applications have classical semantics. Matched-entity synopsis QA inherits the hidden-matching separation between $O(\log N)$ qubits and $Ω(\sqrt{N})$ classical boundary bits. Continual requirements auditing inherits a Max-$k$SAT streaming separation: a recurrent solver uses $O(\log^5 n\log(1/δ))$ qubits and polylogarithmic classical workspace to obtain a $0.7172$-approximation, whereas every classical one-pass finite-information solver attaining that ratio requires $Ω(\sqrt{n})$ coordination width. As a quantum-native compiler test, a stabilizer latent-state dialogue uses $n$ qubits, while every exact finite-state classical causal online realization satisfies $B+M \ge \frac{1}{2}n^2+(\frac{3}{2}-\log_2 3)n+O(1)$. The source protocols, streaming algorithms, and stabilizer witness are imported; the new result is their architecture-independent semantic transfer. These are memory and coordination separations, not runtime or empirical advantages for present-day language models. The stabilizer result assumes exact simulation and ideal noiseless quantum memory.

</details>

### [Quantum steering is equivalent to state-preserving conditional expectations](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10783v1)

**Authors:** Lauritz van Luijk, Amine Marrakchi, Tobias Osborne, Alexander Stottmeister, Henrik Wilming  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.10783v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

Boundary: A completely rational 1+1- dimensional conformal field theory is divided into a region A consisting of two disjoint intervals and their complement B. Haag duality fails in both settings and a normal conditional expectation B′ →A exists, so by Theorem B the relevant question is which state is preserved. For the surface code it is the ground state; for a completely rational chiral conformal net it cannot be the vacuum, because of the Reeh–Schlieder property.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2608.10783_figures/2608.10783_fig2.jpg" alt="Figure 2">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 2.</b> Figure 2: The figure provides an overview of the logical dependencies and the proof structure of the results stated in the introduction. The left-hand column is the general theory of Section 2, which is carried out for normal unital positive maps between JBW*-algebras. the right-hand column provides the basic observations on steering. Theorem B combines the two: Lemma A turns steering into the ensemble extension property, Proposition E turns the latter into a state-preserving conditional expectation. Both items of Theorem C come out of Theorem B via the reduction of mixed states to pure ones.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper establishes a profound connection between the physical notion of quantum steering and abstract mathematics via conditional expectations. It proves that steering is equivalent to the existence of a state-preserving map from one subsystem's algebra ($B'$) onto another ($A$). This result provides powerful tools for analyzing entanglement in complex systems where standard assumptions about local structure break down.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a deep mathematical characterization (via subfactor theory) for a physically intuitive concept (quantum steering), which has implications for understanding entanglement in complex, infinite quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates which fundamental entanglement properties survive in quantum systems with infinitely many degrees of freedom where Haag duality fails. It aims to connect the operational concept of 'quantum steering' to abstract algebraic structures.

**Main result.** For a pure global state, quantum steering is mathematically equivalent to the existence of a state-preserving conditional expectation from the commutant $B'$ onto the algebra $A$.

**Method.** The proof involves establishing equivalences using ensemble decomposition properties and extending these arguments from general Jordan algebras to von Neumann algebras.

**Model / system.** The analysis uses commuting von Neumann algebras $A$ and $B'$, modeling systems where Haag duality fails, such as those found in topologically ordered systems or 1+1-dimensional conformal field theories.

**Key observables.** Quantum steering capability (operational), existence of a state-preserving conditional expectation ($E: B' 	o A$), and the index of the subfactor inclusion $[B':A]$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The nature of the global state ($\omega$) is crucial, often restricted to pure states. The failure of Haag duality ($B' 
eq A$) defines the regime of study.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis frequently restricts itself to pure global states and relies on advanced mathematical machinery from subfactor theory and operator algebras.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates physical examples (like surface codes or CFTs) where Haag duality fails but a conditional expectation might still exist, emphasizing the need for careful state preservation checks.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the equivalence by first defining steering operationally, then linking it to an 'ensemble extension property,' and finally proving this link is equivalent to the existence of the required state-preserving conditional expectation $E: B' 	o A$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In systems with infinitely many degrees of freedom, fundamental results from quantum information theory can fail. An important example is the uniqueness of purifications: Even when two subsystems, described by commuting von Neumann algebras $A$ and $B$, are tomographically complete, purifications of a state on $A$ need not be related by unitaries in $B$. It was recently shown that this occurs precisely when Haag duality fails, i.e., when the commutant $B'$ is strictly larger than $A$. This raises the question of which fundamental entanglement properties survive in such a setting. We show that, for a pure global state, the ability to steer any ensemble decomposition of the marginal state on $A$ by measurements on $B$ is equivalent to the existence of a state-preserving conditional expectation from $B'$ onto $A$. This establishes a direct connection between quantum steering and subfactor theory. The key observation is that steering is equivalent to the existence of extensions of ensemble decompositions from $A$ to $B'$. Working with general Jordan algebras, we prove that unital positive maps have state-preserving left inverses if and only if ensemble decompositions can be lifted. For the inclusion $A\hookrightarrow B'$, a left inverse is precisely a conditional expectation, yielding the characterization above.

</details>

**Authors:** Satvik Singh  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.11000v1>

<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 3.</b> Figure 3: Strong converse and achievability bounds on the classical identification capacity of the qubit depolarizing channel Dp (see Eq. (3.39)) for 0 ≤p ≤1. The light green curve shows the CE converse bounds from Theorem 3.3. The blue curve shows the state-weighted Gaussian converse bound from [25, Theorem 3.8] (see (3.61)). The orange curve shows the Ellipsoid converse bound from [32]. The purple and red curves show the classical capacity [32] and quantum capacity [4] converse bounds, respectively. The dashed curve shows the achievability bound from [14, 31] (see (2.11), (2.20)). Note that the achievability and the CE converse coincide for p ≲0.252 in accordance with Corollary 3.4.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper establishes that the entanglement-assisted transmission capacity ($	ext{CE}$) acts as a strong upper bound for the classical identification capacity ($	ext{CID}$) over noisy quantum channels. While equality holds for low-noise regimes, the authors provide explicit counterexamples showing $	ext{CID} < 	ext{CE}$ for general channels, yielding new insights into the fundamental limits of quantum communication.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous characterization of capacity bounds for information tasks like identification provides fundamental limits on what can be reliably communicated or detected using quantum resources, impacting the design of quantum communication protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper compares the classical identification capacity ($	ext{CID}$) with the entanglement-assisted transmission capacity ($	ext{CE}$) for quantum channels.

**Main result.** It proves that $	ext{CE}$ serves as a strong converse bound for $	ext{CID}$ ($	ext{CID} \le 	ext{CE}$), and demonstrates cases where this bound is strict, leading to the first example of superadditivity for $	ext{CID}$.

**Method.** The proof relies on advanced techniques including the Euclidean geometric method using Gaussian mean width, channel capacity definitions (classical vs. quantum), and constructing specific noisy channels.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes classical message transmission and identification over general noisy quantum channels $N: \mathcal{L}(A) 	o \mathcal{L}(B)$, exemplified by depolarizing and transpose-depolarizing qubits.

**Key observables.** Identification capacity ($	ext{CID}$), Entanglement-assisted transmission capacity ($	ext{CE}$), ordinary transmission capacity ($C$), and quantum identification capacity ($	ext{QID}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Noise parameters ($p, q$) characterizing the specific noisy channels used for testing the bounds.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of reliable coding schemes based on blocklength $n$ and relies on established quantum information theory frameworks.

**Figures summary.** Figure 3 compares various converse and achievability bounds, such as $	ext{CE}$ and $	ext{CID}$, for the qubit depolarizing channel $	ext{D}_p$ across different noise levels.

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines the relevant capacities ($	ext{CID}, 	ext{CE}$) by contrasting classical identification with standard transmission. It then establishes the universal strong converse bound ($	ext{CID} \le 	ext{CE}$), followed by constructing explicit channels where this inequality is strict, and finally discussing conditions under which equality holds.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Classical identification via a noisy channel is a communication task in which the receiver is not required to reconstruct the full transmitted message, but only to decide whether it coincides with a message of interest. This relaxation allows the number of identifiable messages to grow doubly exponentially with the blocklength. For quantum channels, the resulting (doubly exponential) identification capacity $C_{\mathrm{ID}}$ can strictly exceed the ordinary (exponential) transmission capacity $C$.   In this paper, we prove that the entanglement-assisted transmission capacity $C_E$ is a strong converse bound for this task: $C_{\mathrm{ID}}\leq C_E$. For sufficiently low-noise channels, this bound can also be achieved via the Hayden-Winter (quantum) identification + fingerprinting codes. This yields an exact characterization $C_{\mathrm{ID}}=C_E$ of identification capacity for such channels. However, for general channels, we prove that this upper bound can be strict. We exhibit an explicit family of transpose-depolarizing channels for which $C_{\mathrm{ID}}<C_E$. As a consequence, we also obtain the first example of strict superadditivity of the identification capacity $C_{\mathrm{ID}}$.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper studies Unextendible Stabiliser Bases (USBs), which are incomplete sets of orthogonal stabiliser states. Using symplectic geometry, it proves their existence for general qubit and odd-prime qudit systems. The work is significant for showing that while USB unextendibility has deep mathematical consequences (like defining magic witnesses), it does not imply a universal quantitative barrier to distinguishing these states using stabilizer measurements.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides deep mathematical insights into the structure of quantum state spaces defined by stabilizer groups, which is fundamental to understanding quantum error correction codes and entanglement theory in qudit systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates Unextendible Stabiliser Bases (USBs), which are incomplete sets of orthogonal pure stabiliser states analogous to unextendible product bases (UPBs). It aims to characterize their properties, especially concerning resource theories like magic states.

**Main result.** USBs exist for general qubit and qudit systems under certain dimensional constraints. Crucially, the paper shows that USB unextendibility alone does not impose a uniform quantitative obstruction to state discrimination by stabiliser operations.

**Method.** The study employs tools from symplectic geometry applied to Pauli groups in finite fields ($\mathbb{F}_q$). Key techniques include constructing magic witnesses and analyzing the properties of the orthogonal complement subspace relative to UPBs/USBs.

**Model / system.** The model is based on $n$-qudit stabiliser states defined within the framework of the Pauli group structure, analyzed using symplectic geometry. Comparisons are drawn between these stabilizer systems and product bases in quantum information theory.

**Key observables.** Orthogonality of states ($\langle \cdot | \cdot angle$), existence/non-existence of extensions, magic monotone $M(ho)$, and the success probability of stabiliser discrimination protocols ($p_{	ext{SO}}^{	ext{succ}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Local dimension $q$ (especially odd primes vs. $q=2$ for qubits), number of qudits/qubits ($n$), and ancillary system size ($m$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies heavily on the mathematical structure provided by the symplectic form $\omega$ underlying the Pauli group, and comparisons are made assuming specific operational definitions (e.g., distillability).

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the notes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining USBs as analogues of UPBs, providing explicit constructions for small systems (4 qubits, 3 qudits), establishing general existence theorems, and finally comparing resource-theoretic aspects like bound magic properties and discrimination capabilities against known results for UPBs.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study incomplete sets of orthogonal stabiliser states that cannot be extended by a further stabiliser state orthogonal to all of its members. Such sets are the stabiliser analogue of unextendible product bases (UPBs), and we thus call them unextendible stabiliser bases (USBs). Leveraging the symplectic geometry underlying the $n$-qudit Pauli group in prime local dimension, we explicitly construct USBs for systems of four qubits and three qudits of odd prime local dimension. Moreover, we show that these are the respective minimal qubit, respectively qudit numbers for which such bases exist, and that USBs exist for all $n\geq4$ qubit and for all $n\geq3$ odd-prime-dimensional qudit systems. Finally, we compare the resource-theoretic aspects of USBs with those of UPBs. We establish that, analogous to the case of UPBs, the orthogonal complement of every unextendible stabiliser set is a stabiliser-free subspace, and its normalised projector is necessarily magic; moreover, it is bound magic in odd prime dimension, yet need not be for qubits. We also show that USB unextendibility alone imposes no uniform quantitative obstruction to discrimination by stabiliser operations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces 'coupled-layer codes' as a generalization of standard product constructions for building quantum error-correcting codes. By formalizing the coupling using excitation algebras and relating it to homological algebra concepts like mapping cones, the authors demonstrate how to generate non-CSS stabilizer codes. This advances the theoretical understanding of topological quantum computation by providing new methods to construct robust quantum codes.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a powerful mathematical machinery (mapping cones, excitation algebras) to construct novel topological quantum codes that go beyond simple product constructions, which is crucial for realizing fault-tolerant quantum computation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To generalize quantum product constructions for building quantum error-correcting codes beyond standard CSS settings by introducing 'coupled-layer codes.'

**Main result.** The coupled-layer construction provides a general framework to derive non-CSS stabilizer codes, reproducing models like the fermionic toric code and generalizing tensor products via mapping cone structures.

**Method.** The method involves defining an excitation algebra and using an algebra-preserving map to condense Pauli excitations across multiple layers, relating the construction to homological concepts like the mapping cone.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on Quantum Error-Correcting Codes (QECCs), specifically building upon product codes by coupling layers of stabilizer codes. The underlying mathematical structure involves chain complexes and gauging symmetries.

**Key observables.** The resulting quantum error-correcting codes; the ability to produce non-CSS codes; commutation relations between various Pauli operators ($	ext{A}_p^{	ext{q}1}, 	ilde{f}	ext{q}$, etc.).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The structure of the underlying group $G$ and the specific coupling terms derived from the input CSS codes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on algebraic manipulations concerning overlaps between supports of Pauli operators, assuming certain commutation relations hold for simplification.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the tensor product complex structure (Fig. 1), the general coupled-layer construction via gauging (Fig. 2 & 3), and the mathematical formalism using mapping cones to represent code products and surgeries (Fig. 4, 5, 6).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining product codes to introducing the generalized coupled-layer concept. It then details the connection to gauging symmetries and homological algebra via mapping cones, culminating in detailed algebraic proofs of commutation relations for the resulting operators.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Product codes are an important class of quantum error-correcting codes constructed from multiple input codes, which can give rise to asymptotically good quantum low-density parity check codes. In previous work, we showed how the product between two codes can be physically implemented by coupling layers of the first code using checks of the second code. In this work, we further unify product code constructions with coupled-layer constructions of phases of matter by introducing coupled-layer codes. The essential strategy is to condense general excitations created by Pauli operators among multiple decoupled layers of the first code. The condensation is specified by an excitation algebra, which encodes the excitations, along with an algebra-preserving map. This coupling between layers generalizes the notion of gauging in physics as well as the mapping cone in homological algebra, and can be used to produce non-CSS codes. As examples, we show how coupled-layer codes reproduce the X-cube and Chamon models. We further generalize the balanced product code by allowing a unitary transformation in addition to a group action by free permutation and describe its corresponding coupled-layer construction. In particular, we show how balancing by a ZX-duality can reproduce non-CSS codes such as the fermionic toric code and the 3-fermion Walker-Wang model in 3D.

</details>

**Authors:** Evagoras Stylianou, Vinayak Ramkumar, Holger Boche, Rawad Bitar  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.10912v1>

**Summary.** This paper develops the theory and explicit constructions for Quantum Locally Recoverable Codes (qLRCs) characterized by parameters $(r, t, x)$. It shows that these quantum codes can be derived from classical LRCs via the CSS construction. The main contribution is deriving tight upper bounds on the code rate and dimension, alongside providing concrete families of such codes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools and concrete constructions for advanced quantum error correcting codes that go beyond standard stabilizer or distance-based requirements by incorporating locality constraints.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work focuses on constructing and deriving bounds for quantum locally recoverable codes (qLRCs) with specific parameters $(r, t, x)$. It establishes the necessary and sufficient conditions for a CSS code to achieve this structure.

**Main result.** Explicit families of binary dual-containing classical LRCs are constructed using subset-inclusion matrices, which yield corresponding quantum qLRCs. Upper bounds on the dimension and rate for these codes are derived.

**Method.** The authors utilize the CSS construction to translate known results from classical locally recoverable codes (cLRCs) into the quantum domain. They derive bounds by analyzing puncturing techniques applied to the underlying classical code structure.

**Model / system.** The model is Quantum Error Correction, specifically focusing on $(r, t, x)$-qLRCs built via the CSS construction from binary linear codes $C$. The parameters involve locality ($r$), availability ($t$), and intersection parameter ($x$).

**Key observables.** Code dimension ($\kappa$), code rate (R), minimum distance ($\delta$), and the specific parameters $(r, t, x)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Locality $r$, availability $t$, intersection parameter $x$, and the underlying classical code structure defined by parameters $(m, s, \alpha)$ in the construction.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption for the necessary and sufficient condition is that the underlying classical codes have a dual minimum distance of at least two. The constructions also rely on the resulting classical code being dual-containing.

**Figures summary.** TABLE I lists representative parameters $(m, s, \alpha)$ satisfying the required conditions and provides the corresponding quantum code parameters $[[n, \kappa, \delta]]_q$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the equivalence between CSS qLRCs and cLRCs (Theorem 1). It then details a construction method using subset-inclusion matrices to generate explicit cLRCs. Finally, it derives general upper bounds and specific Singleton-like dimension bounds for the resulting quantum codes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, we study $(r,t,x)$ quantum locally recoverable codes (qLRCs) with locality $r$, $t$ recovery sets per qudit, and intersection parameter $x$. We first show that, assuming the underlying classical codes have dual minimum distance at least two, a CSS code is an $(r,t,x)$-qLRC if and only if the underlying classical codes are $(r,t,x)$ classical LRCs (cLRCs) with common recovery sets. We then use subset-inclusion matrices to construct families of binary dual-containing $(r,t,x)$-cLRCs, which yield binary $(r,t,x)$-qLRCs via the CSS construction. For CSS $(r,t,x)$-qLRCs, we derive upper bounds on the dimension and rate, minimum-distance bounds in the pure case, and a Singleton-like dimension bound in the exact case. Finally, we show that these families attain high rates and nontrivial minimum distances.

</details>

### [Exact Expressions of Entropy for Classical Non-interacting Many-body Systems](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11104v1)

**Authors:** Yuheng Wu, Henrik Heelweg, Rigel Galgana  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.11104v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

</figure>

**Summary.** This work develops a universal statistical mechanics theory to calculate the exact entropy for finite classical many-body systems across all relevant ensembles. By applying maximum entropy principles based on two core physical postulates, the authors show that while ensemble properties differ at small sizes, they converge correctly to established thermodynamic limits. This provides a rigorous framework for understanding finite-size effects in statistical physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical systems, establishing rigorous, non-asymptotic ensemble equivalence is foundational. The methodology of deriving fundamental quantities from minimal postulates (stationarity/unbiasedness) has parallels in defining quantum equilibrium states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a universal ensemble theory that provides exact expressions for entropy for classical non-interacting many-body systems, resolving discrepancies in thermodynamic properties among different ensembles when the system is finite.

**Main result.** The derived entropy expressions across various statistical ensembles are shown to converge asymptotically to a common limit in the thermodynamic limit, confirming ensemble equivalence while rigorously quantifying finite-size corrections.

**Method.** The theory uses maximum entropy optimization based on two postulates: stationarity and unbiasedness, applied to four representative thermodynamic ensembles.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as classical non-interacting many-body particles governed by a simple quadratic Hamiltonian (L = sum(p_i^2/2m)).

**Key observables.** Entropy (S), Temperature (T), Pressure (P), Chemical Potential (mu), and the ensemble-dependent intensive variables.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size (N, V), Energy (E), and the thermodynamic limit ($N 	o \infty$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The system is assumed to be a regular Hamiltonian system, and the analysis relies on extending beyond the standard thermodynamic limit approximation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematics illustrate the state space constraints for four representative ensembles. Figure 2 illustrates the ensemble dependence of intensive variables like chemical potential ($\partial S / \partial N$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the need for a universal theory to address finite-size effects, then derives exact entropy expressions for four key ensembles using maximum entropy principles, and finally proves the asymptotic equivalence of these results in the thermodynamic limit.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In the thermodynamic limit, the equilibrium state of a many-body system can be characterized by three pairs of conjugate thermodynamic variables: $E/T,V/P,N/μ$. In this limit, the thermodynamic properties in all ensembles are equivalent up to the leading order of $E,V,N$. However, for systems of finite size, this ensemble equivalence is no longer exact, and the thermodynamic properties may differ substantially among ensembles. To quantify these finite-size effects rigorously, it is desirable to develop a universal ensemble theory applicable to systems of arbitrary size, providing exact expressions for entropy and, thereby, giving rise to the precise value of all equilibrium thermodynamic quantities. In this work, we propose a theory that determines the exact entropy expressions for classical non-interacting many-body systems of arbitrary size across all statistical ensembles, based on only two postulates: \textbf{stationarity}, requiring that the physical laws be invariant under time translation, and \textbf{unbiasedness}, requiring that the equilibrium mixed state maximize the entropy subject to the prescribed constraints. Moreover, we show that the entropy expressions obtained in different ensembles converge to the common asymptotic form $S \asymp \ln\!\left( \left( \frac{4πm e E}{3N} \right)^{3N/2} \cdot \left(\frac{V}{N}\right)^N \right)+N$, consistent with the predictions of the large deviation theory.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes how reusable design patterns are actually implemented in open-source quantum software by building advanced mining tools. It extends pattern detection to composition analysis using graph databases to map component call structures. This work provides a critical resource (qpa pipeline and knowledge base) for understanding the practical adoption and architectural evolution of quantum algorithms.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about physical Hamiltonians, understanding the software architecture and limitations of quantum programming tools is crucial for developing scalable algorithms that can be physically realized or simulated efficiently.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research moves beyond simple detection of quantum software patterns to analyze their composition, understanding how high-level abstractions are assembled in practice.

**Main result.** The authors successfully built a pipeline that analyzes pattern usage across 80 open-source projects and constructed composition graphs showing the internal assembly logic of these patterns within quantum frameworks.

**Method.** They developed an enhanced mining tool (qpa) that uses multiple matching channels and vocabulary expansion, coupled with building graph databases to map component call relationships.

**Model / system.** The focus is on quantum software development using existing frameworks (like Qiskit), treating the framework components as the system being analyzed rather than a physical Hamiltonian or state.

**Key observables.** Pattern occurrence frequency, detection accuracy (micro-F1 score), and call relationships within composition graphs (direct vs. internal calls).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The catalog size (61 patterns) and the number of analyzed projects (80).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that framework components can be reliably mapped to abstract quantum software patterns, and that LLMs can semi-automatically classify new components.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for composition analysis over simple detection, detailing the enhancement of the mining tool (qpa), presenting quantitative accuracy results, and finally using composition graphs to analyze pattern assembly logic.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum software patterns provide high-level abstractions for building quantum programs, but there is still little empirical evidence on how they are adopted in practice. In prior work, we extended an existing quantum-pattern atlas into a 61-pattern catalog, created a knowledge base that links framework components to those patterns, and built a tool that mines pattern implementations from open-source code. We applied this tool on 80 projects and find that all 23 patterns occur in practice. In this work, we extend the tool with two additional matching channels and a vocabulary expansion step, and execute a quantitative evaluation of its accuracy on Qrisp, a framework not present in the knowledge base, reaching a micro-F1 of 0.712 against 0.449 without the expansion step. We then construct composition graphs that record calls among the high-level framework components associated with patterns and store them in a graph database. We use these graphs to examine how pattern implementations are assembled inside each framework, why patterns co-occur, and how much of a pattern's detection count comes from components called directly by developers rather than introduced through internal framework calls. We release qpa, an open-source mining pipeline, together with the knowledge base, which maps 286 framework components across five sources to the pattern catalog, maintained with the support of an LLM ensemble that classifies newly extracted components, and the resulting pattern usage dataset, to support reproducible studies on the adoption and evolution of quantum patterns.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates using quantum computing algorithms, specifically QSVT and HHL, to solve the massive linear systems generated by simulating Maxwell's equations via FDTD. The research validates these approaches on industrial electromagnetics problems, showing that QSVT is superior and that resource scaling remains manageable even for very large physical domains.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly applies advanced quantum linear algebra techniques (QSVT/HHL) to a canonical problem in computational physics (electromagnetics), providing concrete benchmarks for near-term quantum advantage claims in applied science.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is accelerating computationally intensive simulations of electromagnetics, specifically solving large linear systems derived from Maxwell's equations.

**Main result.** The study demonstrates that the Quantum Singular Value Transformation (QSVT) algorithm consistently achieves higher accuracy than HHL for these tasks. Crucially, it shows that the system matrix condition number saturates with increasing spatial grid size, allowing scaling to realistic industrial dimensions without prohibitive resource growth.

**Method.** The authors map the time-stepping evolution of the Finite-Difference Time-Domain (FDTD) method into a linear system ($	ilde{A}\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}$) and then apply quantum algorithms like HHL and QSVT to solve this system efficiently.

**Model / system.** The physical model is electromagnetics governed by Maxwell's equations, simulated using the FDTD method on lattices. The mathematical core involves solving the resulting large, sparse linear system matrix $	ilde{A}$.

**Key observables.** Quantum-state fidelity ($\epsilon_F$), algorithmic success probability ($P_s$), and the condition number ($\kappa$) of the system matrix.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The required qubits scale logarithmically with spatial size and time steps; performance is critically dependent on $\kappa$ and the approximation degree $d$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Simplifications include assuming lossless materials, frequency-independent $\epsilon$ and $\mu$, and using Perfectly Matched Layers (PML) boundary conditions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematics of field formulations (Yee lattice), circuit diagrams for HHL/QSVT, and plots showing the condition number's independence from large lattice sizes ($L_x$), as well as performance metrics vs. lattice size.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical problem (Maxwell's equations via FDTD) and reformulates it into a linear system. It then details the application of quantum algorithms (HHL, QSVT) to solve this system, benchmarking them across industrial use cases like radar propagation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Computational electromagnetics plays a central role in many industrial applications but often requires substantial computational resources, particularly when fine spatial discretizations are needed. While classical approaches remain the standard, quantum computing offers the potential to accelerate large-scale simulations by encoding them with a limited number of qubits.   Here, we investigate the performance and resource scaling of the Harrow-Hassidim-Lloyd (HHL) and Quantum Singular Value Transformation (QSVT) algorithms for solving linear systems generated by the finite-difference time-domain (FDTD) method, a widely adopted numerical scheme for discretizing Maxwell's equations. We benchmark their performance across representative industrial use cases, including radar propagation, lens simulations, and beamforming processes. Our results demonstrate the validity of the approaches, achieving state infidelities smaller than $2\cdot 10^{-3}$ with success probabilities greater than $10^{-3}$, compatible with practical quantum state sampling. Overall, we observe that the QSVT method consistently delivers higher accuracy.   We further observe that the condition number of the linear matrix, a key factor governing the performance of quantum solvers, saturates as the number of spatial lattice points increases. This implies that the spatial grid can be scaled to realistic industrial dimensions without increasing the HHL or QSVT circuit depth due to ill-conditioned matrices.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel method to verify Full Quantum Network Nonlocality (FQNN) across any network topology using just one nonlinear Bell-like inequality. The technique involves augmenting the original network with auxiliary local sources and testing the resulting structure against a derived inequality. A violation of this single test proves that all original quantum sources exhibit genuine nonlocality.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, general tool for certifying fundamental quantum properties (nonlocality) in complex, realistic network architectures, which is highly relevant to quantum communication protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop a method to verify Full Quantum Network Nonlocality (FQNN) in quantum networks with arbitrary topologies.

**Main result.** The authors propose an efficient technique that certifies FQNN using only a single nonlinear Bell-like inequality, overcoming limitations of previous topology-specific tests.

**Method.** They enlarge the original network by adding carefully chosen auxiliary local sources and parties. The resulting augmented network is then tested with a single derived $l$-QNL inequality.

**Model / system.** The system involves quantum networks distributing entangled states among spatially separated parties ($S_1, S_2, \dots$). Specific examples analyzed include triangular, chain, and cyclic network topologies ($\Xi(n, m)$).

**Key observables.** Violation of the derived nonlinear Bell-like inequality (e.g., $|	ext{Inew}|_1/4 + |	ext{Jnew}|_1/4 > 	ext{Bound}$), which implies that all original sources must be nonlocal.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The maximum independent number ($h$) and the topological parameters defining the network structure are crucial for constructing the inequality.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that violating the derived inequality provides a conclusive proof of FQNN, and concrete calculations sometimes assume sources are in Werner states.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the concept using triangular networks (showing augmentation), chain networks, and cyclic networks, detailing how auxiliary local sources expand the original structure for testing.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a general inequality based on network topology parameters ($h$) and then applies this method to specific, complex topologies like $\Xi(3, 3)$, chain networks ($\Xi(n, n-1)$), and cyclic networks ($\Xi(n, n)$) through augmentation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Full quantum network nonlocality (FQNN) describes a scenario where all sources in a network are nonlocal. Existing criteria of FQNN can only be verified in star networks by violating a single Bell-like inequality. Here we propose a method that certifies FQNN in general quantum networks using only a single Bell-like inequality. We show that the topological obstacle to one-shot detection can be overcome by expanding the original network with a carefully chosen number of auxiliary local sources and parties. The correlations of the enlarged network are then tested with a single inequality; a violation implies that all original sources must be nonlocal. Our approach provides an efficient, experimentally friendly way to verify FQNN in any network topology.

</details>

**Summary.** The research develops a method to extract a purely geometric component of energy pumping from an exciton manifold by analyzing its energy transfer statistics via Full Counting Statistics. By implementing optimized, non-uniform driving protocols, the authors demonstrate a significant enhancement in signal detectability while proving the robustness of this geometric signature against realistic environmental noise.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, measurable signature—the geometric contribution to energy transfer statistics—in a solid-state platform. The methodology for separating this topological/geometric feature from thermal noise using advanced counting statistics is highly relevant to open quantum system dynamics in condensed matter.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work aims to generate and detect a geometric contribution to energy transfer statistics (geometric phonon pumping) from slow cyclic modulation in an open quantum system, separating it from dynamic noise.

**Main result.** A Non-Uniform Field Modulation (NUFM) driving protocol significantly enhances the signal-to-noise ratio by suppressing dynamical fluctuations while robustly retaining the geometric pumping signal across realistic environmental variations.

**Method.** The analysis employs Full Counting Statistics (FCS) applied to a master equation framework, separating energy cumulants into dynamic and geometric parts based on loop traversal direction reversal.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as a gate-tunable WSe2/WS2 moiré exciton manifold, treated as an open quantum system governed by a Liouvillian approach. The dynamics are analyzed using concepts derived from the tilted master equation and adiabatic approximations.

**Key observables.** Geometric first cumulant ($C_{	ext{geo},1}$), dynamic mean ($	ext{Cdyn}_1$), cycle-to-cycle fluctuation ($C_2$), and the fixed-time intrinsic Signal-to-Noise Ratio (SNR).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate voltage modulation rate, pump intensity modulation rate, environmental relaxation rates (phonon/radiative loss), and the cycle period ($	au$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the adiabatic approximation for slow driving and models coupling to the environment via phenomenological rate terms rather than fully coherent Hamiltonian terms.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare performance metrics (like SNR) between uniform vs. NUFM traversals, show period dependence of signal retention, and illustrate the local mechanism involving relaxation gaps and control parameter rates.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework using FCS for open systems, develops the geometric separation technique via loop reversal, optimizes the driving protocol by comparing uniform versus non-uniform modulation (NUFM), and finally validates the robustness of the enhanced signal against varying physical parameters.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Slow cyclic modulation of an open quantum system can generate a geometric contribution to its energy transfer statistics, separable from the dynamic background. In this work we construct an experimentally anchored four state open system model for a gate-tunable WSe2/WS2 moiré exciton manifold within a population level full counting statistics framework. We first drive the system uniformly through a closed gate and pump loop and separate the geometric contribution from the dynamic background by reversing the loop direction. We then examine how this response changes with environmental rates, exciton coupling, control conditions, and static spectral broadening. A shorter cycle period followed by nonuniform traversal of the driving loop substantially improves detectability while preserving the geometric response. Combined with enhanced phonon relaxation and reduced radiative loss, this gives about a fourteenfold fixed time signal to noise gain relative to the uniform loop. Our results show that the geometric phonon response remains robust against realistic variations in the system and its environment, and that we can improve its detectability more effectively by suppressing dynamical noise through frequency modulated driving.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors studied graphene's optical response in ultrahigh magnetic fields, finding that the behavior deviates significantly from simple models. They demonstrated that at extreme fields, the system forms a fully compensated electron-hole plasma whose collective excitations are accurately described by an Alfvén wave model. This work establishes graphene as a platform for simulating astrophysical relativistic plasmas.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges condensed matter physics (graphene) with high-energy astrophysics concepts (relativistic plasma), using advanced magneto-optics to probe fundamental quantum electrodynamic effects in a solid-state system.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate the infrared magneto-optical response of n-doped epitaxial graphene under ultrahigh magnetic fields, specifically determining if the system deviates from ideal Dirac cone behavior.

**Main result.** The extreme-field absorption spectra are excellently reproduced by a collective Alfvén wave model, demonstrating that the 2D graphene system can realize a tabletop analog to relativistic electron-positron plasmas.

**Method.** A combination of experimental magneto-optical spectroscopy (using single-turn coils and flux compression) and theoretical modeling based on generalized bilayer graphene Hamiltonians was employed.

**Model / system.** The physical system is n-doped epitaxial graphene on 4H-SiC, modeled using effective Hamiltonians that account for band warping and electron-hole plasma dynamics in extreme magnetic fields. The analysis transitions from single-particle cyclotron resonance to collective Alfvén wave excitations.

**Key observables.** Anomalously broad magneto-absorption spectra, 'camel-back' structure in ARPES dispersion, critical level crossing between $N=0^+$ and $N=0^-$ Landau levels.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic field up to 560 T; energy gap $E_g \sim 0.2$ eV; magnetic enhancement of sublattice potential asymmetry (0.10 eV to 0.12 eV).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the high-field behavior is governed by collective plasma modes rather than single-particle cyclotron resonance, necessitating the use of a bilayer-derived model for LL calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows magneto-transmission spectra using various lasers; Figure 2 displays ARPES data showing the 'camel-back' structure; and Figure 3 illustrates calculated Landau Level fan charts predicting level crossing near 160–200 T.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first observing anomalous optical responses, then using ARPES to map a distorted Dirac dispersion, theoretically modeling the high-field LL behavior showing a critical crossover, and finally interpreting the extreme-field spectra via the collective Alfvén wave model.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the infrared magneto-optical response of n-doped epitaxial graphene on 4H-SiC in ultrahigh magnetic fields up to 560~T, utilizing single-turn coil and electromagnetic flux compression techniques. The measured absorption spectra are anomalously broad, strongly deviating from conventional cyclotron resonance. Angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES) reveals a distorted Dirac dispersion featuring a ``camel-back" structure with an energy gap of $E_g \sim 0.2$~eV. Using the band parameters extracted via a generalized bilayer graphene model, we construct a Landau level (LL) fan chart that dictates a critical level crossing between the $N=0^+$ and $N=0^-$ states near 160--200~T. At this threshold, the optical transition mechanism undergoes a dramatic shift from an electron-dominated collective mode to a cooperative electron-hole collective excitation. Furthermore, the extreme-field absorption spectra under thulium fiber laser excitation ($\hbarω_0 = 0.636$~eV)---culminating in a massive resonance near 400~T with a shoulder at 200~T---are excellently reproduced by a collective Alfvén wave model. This analysis also evidences a magnetic-field-induced enhancement of the sublattice potential asymmetry parameter (from 0.10~eV to 0.12~eV). This directly signifies that the macroscopic electron-hole band asymmetry is further amplified by the applied magnetic field. Ultimately, the field-induced energy inversion generates a strongly interacting, fully compensated electron-hole plasma. The resonant excitation of Alfvén waves in this regime demonstrates that a pristine tabletop analog to the relativistic electron-positron plasmas found in astrophysical extremes can be elegantly realized within a 2D graphene system.

</details>

### [Moving Analogue Horizons in Stationary Ferroelectrics](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10371v1)

**Authors:** David Galvez-Poblete, Rubén M. Otxoa, Alvaro S. Nunez, Sebastian Allende  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2608.10371v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `analog quantum simulation` **4/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **2/5** · `Frenkel-Kontorova` **1/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `superradiant laser` **1/5**

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that ferroelectric materials can act as novel platforms for analogue gravity by exhibiting effective flow in their polarization dynamics. The key mechanism involves modulating the material's stiffness, which creates mobile analogue horizons separating different excitation regimes. Superradiant amplification is predicted to be the primary observable signature of this phenomenon.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between condensed matter physics (ferroelectrics) and analogue gravity provides a tangible, solid-state platform for studying fundamental concepts of spacetime dynamics using controllable material parameters.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate analogue gravity phenomena, specifically mobile analogue horizons, using ferroelectric materials where the effective flow is induced by modulating material parameters rather than physical medium transport.

**Main result.** Ferroelectrics provide a novel and experimentally controllable platform for studying massive scalar excitations in analogue gravity, with superradiant-like amplification being a key observable signature.

**Method.** The analysis models small polarization fluctuations (ferrons) using a massive Klein-Gordon equation modified by an induced flow term, comparing it to effective metrics derived from curved spacetime analogues.

**Model / system.** The system is a one-dimensional ferroelectric described by the Landau-Ginzburg-Devonshire formalism. Dynamics are governed by a massive Klein-Gordon equation with flow: $(\partial_t - u\partial_\xi)^2 \phi - v^2 \partial_\xi^2 \phi + m^2 \phi = 0$.

**Key observables.** Analogue horizons (where $u=v$), superradiant-like amplification, and the effective mass gap ($\omega_{gap}$)

**Important parameters / regimes.** Modulation velocity ($u$), ferronic velocity ($v$), external electric field strength (tuning $m^2$)

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a local approximation derived from the adiabaticity condition ($\epsilon \ll 1$), requiring modulation wavelengths to be much larger than the characteristic ferronic length scale.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the dynamic modulation of the ferronic velocity and marks the location of the analogue horizon.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model using LGD theory, derives the flowing Klein-Gordon equation, analyzes the resulting effective metric components to identify horizons based on flow vs. material speed ($u$ vs $v$), and discusses observable phenomena like superradiance and tunability via external fields.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that a traveling modulation of the polarization-gradient stiffness in a ferroelectric material induces an effective flow in its collective polarization dynamics. Within a controlled local approximation, small polarization fluctuations, or ferrons, obey a massive Klein-Gordon equation with flow. Unlike conventional analogue-gravity platforms, the effective flow originates from the modulation of the material parameters rather than from the physical transport of the medium. This mechanism enables mobile analogue horizons separating sub-ferronic and super-ferronic regions, the latter supporting negative-norm antiferron modes. Their coupling to positive-norm ferrons gives rise to superradiant-like amplification, while the effective mass gap can be tuned independently through an external electric field. Ferroelectric systems therefore provide a novel and experimentally controllable platform for analogue gravity with massive scalar excitations.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper reports an advanced experimental technique to stabilize silicon double quantum dots against disruptive low-frequency charge noise using a fast, multi-parameter feedback loop implemented via FPGA and RF-reflectometry. This method significantly increases the effective feedback bandwidth to 5 kHz, which is crucial for maintaining qubit coherence in solid-state quantum computing architectures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses a critical engineering bottleneck in solid-state quantum computing: decoherence due to environmental charge noise. The implementation of real-time, high-bandwidth feedback control is highly relevant for building scalable quantum processors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is actively stabilizing quantum dot potentials against low-frequency 1/f charge noise to enable long-term operation of semiconductor spin qubits.

**Main result.** The high-speed feedback scheme successfully suppressed low-frequency 1/f noise, achieving a -6 dB noise suppression up to a bandwidth of 5 kHz.

**Method.** The authors extended the Gradient-Descent Feedback (GDFB) approach by integrating high-bandwidth RF-reflectometry readout with an FPGA controller for continuous multi-parameter gradient calculation and fast gate voltage updates.

**Model / system.** The experiment is performed on a Silicon Double Quantum Dot (DQD) fabricated on a heterostructure, which serves as the physical platform for semiconductor spin qubits.

**Key observables.** Low-frequency 1/f charge noise, and the in-phase component of the reflected microwave signal used to measure potential shifts.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The achieved noise suppression bandwidth is 5 kHz, significantly surpassing previous limits based on DC transport measurements. The feedback cycle time dictates a theoretical maximum cutoff frequency of $\sim 6.1 \text{ kHz}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitation noted is that the current feedback bandwidth is constrained by the total feedback cycle time ($\approx 81.8 \mu\text{s}$ per cycle).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the experimental setup, show Power Spectral Density (PSD) comparisons demonstrating noise suppression with and without feedback, and plot the measured attenuation of the PSD as a function of perturbation frequency.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the necessity of active stabilization for spin qubits, introduces the advanced GDFB protocol using RF-reflectometry on an FPGA platform, presents results showing high-bandwidth noise suppression, and concludes by discussing future work related to coherence time characterization.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Long term operation of semiconductor spin qubits requires active stabilization of quantum dot potentials against low-frequency charge noise. Previous work demonstrated a gradient-descent feedback (GDFB) approach on a silicon double quantum dot utilizing transport current measurements. We extend such a GDFB approach in a silicon double quantum dot device with high-bandwidth rf-reflectometry readout by utilizing a field-programmable gate array, the OPX by Quantum Machines, for digital signal processing. The OPX enables continuous multi-parameter gradient calculation and quick gate voltage updates, significantly increasing the effective feedback bandwidth compared to previous work. By operating with 8 steps per feedback cycle and an integration time of 25.6 $μ$s, this high speed stabilization scheme achieves a -6 dB noise suppression up to a bandwidth of 5 kHz. This effectively suppresses low frequency 1/f noise, maintaining device stability over longer time periods, and potentially enables real-time control in large-scale quantum dot arrays.

</details>

**Summary.** The research uses cathodoluminescence from plasmonic nanostructures to probe electron beam coherence. It experimentally demonstrates that while single electrons passing sequentially through multiple scatterers exhibit clear interference patterns, using a broad electron beam averages over these paths, resulting only in an incoherent sum. The work highlights the critical role of preserving quantum path information for observing coherent quantum effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges quantum optics/coherence concepts into solid-state excitation physics using electron beams. The necessity of erasing which-path information echoes fundamental principles in quantum measurement theory, making it relevant for understanding coherence limits in nanoscale spectroscopy.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how electron beam coherence affects cathodoluminescence (CL) signals when exciting plasmonic nanostructures, specifically comparing interference effects from single-electron paths versus extended beam excitation.

**Main result.** Interference patterns are observed when the electron passes coherently through multiple separated plasmonic scatterers within the evanescent field; however, these interferences are lost when using a widely defocused (extended) electron beam spot.

**Method.** Angle-resolved cathodoluminescence (AR CL) measurements were performed on thin films and double plasmonic pillars using scanning electron microscopes, complemented by Fourier transform analysis of spectral data.

**Model / system.** The experiments use energetic electrons exciting $	ext{Si}_3	ext{N}_4$ films or gold-coated nanopillars. The theoretical framework contrasts the incoherent sum expected from extended beams with the coherent interference observed when path information is preserved (e.g., passing between pillars).

**Key observables.** Angular CL emission profile, $	ext{k}_\parallel$-space images showing interference fringes, and time-of-flight components derived from Fourier transforms.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Electron beam spot size ($\sim 4 	ext{ nm}$ vs. $\mu	ext{m}$), plasmonic pillar separation, and the ability to correlate CL emission with post-selected electron states.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The nonrecoil approximation is used in theoretical calculations; a key limitation noted is that extended beams inherently erase which-path information necessary for observing interference.

**Figures summary.** Figures show AR CL data comparing focused vs. spread beam excitation (incoherent sum); they also display complex interference patterns and Fourier transforms from double plasmonic pillars, identifying components related to pillar heights.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the contrast between incoherent summation over extended beams (using thin films) and coherent interference observed with single-electron paths (using separated scatterers). It then details measurements on double pillars, confirming coherence via TOF analysis, and concludes by proposing correlation measurements to fully retrieve path-dependent interference.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Energetic electrons create distinct cathodoluminescence (CL) angular distributions upon interaction with dielectric and plasmonic nanostructures, providing valuable information on coherences in the excitation pathways. A counterintuitive prediction is that the CL signals arising from the excitation associated with different lateral regions of an extended electron wave are mutually incoherent and do not interfere, while the signals originating from different structures within the electromagnetic field of a narrow electron beam are mutually coherent. We present conclusive experimental evidence of these effects by examining the angular CL emission profile from defocused electron-beam excitation of a thin silicon nitride film, which is shown to follow an incoherent sum of CL excitations within the electron beam spot. In contrast, CL interferences are observed for separated plasmonic scatterers excited within the evanescent field of a single electron. Coherence may be recovered through correlations between emitted light and post-selected electron states, for which we propose a measurement geometry that erases which-path information of the electron trajectory.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that amorphous carbon (MAC) membranes can function as highly sensitive nanoelectromechanical resonators, opening a new platform for studying mechanics governed by structural disorder. By analyzing nonlinear resonance features like Duffing responses and mode coupling, the authors establish MAC's utility for investigating complex dynamics in the 2D amorphous limit.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical mechanics, the study of disorder-governed dynamics, nonlinearity, and mode coupling in low-dimensional systems provides experimental analogs for understanding dissipation and emergent behavior in open quantum systems or disordered many-body models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to extend mechanical resonator research from crystalline 2D materials (like graphene) to the amorphous 2D limit using monolayer amorphous carbon (MAC). This explores how structural disorder affects nanoscale mechanics.

**Main result.** MAC membranes are established as a robust nanoelectromechanical platform, exhibiting pronounced nonlinear dynamics including hardening/softening and intermodal coupling at low effective pretension.

**Method.** The research combines optothermal actuation with sensitive interferometric readout to characterize the mechanical resonance and nonlinear response of suspended MAC nanodrums.

**Model / system.** The physical system is atomically thin, amorphous carbon (MAC) membranes suspended over substrates. The analysis models the dynamics using a tensioned membrane description and incorporates optical transduction via reflection from the air-membrane-vacuum gap stack.

**Key observables.** Resonant frequencies ($\sim 13.9$ MHz), Quality factors ($Q$), nonlinear Duffing responses (hardening/softening), and mode spectra showing intermodal coupling.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low effective pretension regime ($10^{-4} - 10^{-3} 	ext{ N m}^{-1}$), which amplifies geometric nonlinearities and stress heterogeneity effects.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a transfer-matrix model for optical reflection and assumes the system can be described by modified Duffing oscillator equations, though deviations suggest more complex coupling.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate structural characterization (Raman/AFM), linear dynamic response spectra showing $Q$ distribution, and mode spectra demonstrating nonlinear behavior like internal resonance dips.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical platform (MAC membranes) to characterizing its fundamental linear dynamics, then delving into complex nonlinear regimes by analyzing frequency-response curves and multi-mode spectral signatures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Atomically thin mechanical resonators have been realized predominantly in crystalline two-dimensional (2D) materials, such as graphene, where long-range crystalline order sets their elastic properties and defines their nonlinear resonant behavior. Extending these concepts to the amorphous 2D limit has remained largely unexplored. Here, we demonstrate that monolayer amorphous carbon (MAC) forms suspended membranes that support optothermal actuation and sensitive interferometric readout across both linear and nonlinear regimes of its resonant motion. We resolve thermomechanical motion, driven resonances, and multimode spectra in MAC nanodrums. The frequencies of fundamental vibration modes correspond to unusually low pretensions, placing monolayer MAC nanodrums in a regime where geometric nonlinearities, stress heterogeneity, and mode coupling emerge at comparatively low drive powers. Consistently, we observe pronounced nonlinear dynamics, including hardening, softening, and mixed Duffing responses, nonlinear damping, parametrically excited modes, and signatures of intermodal coupling. These results establish MAC as a robust nanoelectromechanical platform and open an experimental route to disorder-governed nanomechanics in the 2D amorphous limit.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically demonstrates how to create highly confined, degenerate bound states in the continuum (h-BICs) within photonic crystal structures beyond their normal diffraction limits. By leveraging $C_{6v}$ symmetry and tuning structural parameters, the authors suppress radiation into multiple diffraction channels simultaneously. This opens pathways for designing novel optical devices with enhanced light-matter interaction governed by topological physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems because it deals with engineered photonic band gaps (BICs) that exhibit extreme confinement and topological properties. The concept of controlling leakage rates via symmetry protection is a key tool in designing high-quality optical cavities for quantum applications.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to demonstrate the existence of degenerate hybrid bound states in the continuum (h-BICs) possessing high-order topological charges, specifically operating beyond the standard diffraction limit.

**Main result.** A novel mechanism is proposed and shown to realize at-Gamma degenerate h-BICs by combining symmetry protection for the zeroth order and parameter tuning to eliminate radiation into first-order channels. The frequency splitting near degeneracy scales quadratically and isotropically.

**Method.** The study employs an effective Hamiltonian approach, derived from analyzing band structures in photonic crystals, combined with topological charge calculations via winding numbers.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a Photonic Crystal (PhC) slab exhibiting $C_{6v}$ symmetry. The analysis uses an effective Hamiltonian $	ilde{H}_{	ext{eff}}$ whose eigenvalues govern the frequency and leakage rate dispersion ($\omega \pm i\gamma$) around the degeneracy point.

**Key observables.** Q-factor ($Q$), topological charges (V points), band structure, and far-field polarization maps.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is performed in the regime beyond the diffraction limit, characterized by geometric parameters $	ilde{r}$ and $	ilde{d}$, and momentum $|\mathbf{k}_\parallel|$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption involves using an effective Hamiltonian model to approximate the band structure near the degeneracy point, which shows good agreement for small in-plane wavevectors ($|\mathbf{k}_\parallel| < 0.02$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of h-BIC formation mechanisms, the degenerate band structure and Q-factor distribution at $\Gamma$, and the dependence of the Q-factor on structural parameters ($	ilde{r}, 	ilde{d}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical system (PhC slab) and the theoretical framework using an effective Hamiltonian. It then details the mechanism for achieving degeneracy by suppressing radiation in multiple diffraction channels, followed by numerical validation of the scaling laws and topological features.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We demonstrate the existence of at-$Γ$ degenerate hybrid bound-states in the continuum (BICs) with high-order topological charges and quadratic band degeneracy in periodic photonic structures with $C_{6v}$ symmetry above the diffraction limit. The degenerate BICs are realized by combining symmetry protection, which suppresses radiation into zeroth-order diffraction channel, with quadratic band degeneracy and parameter tuning to eliminate radiation into first-order diffraction channels. An effective Hamiltonian is used to investigate the band structure around the degeneracy point, and the topological dynamics in all diffraction channels are characterized. This work reveals a novel mechanism for realizing degenerate BICs above the diffraction limit, opening up opportunities for new physics and applications.

</details>

### [Quadrupolar phase transition in superconducting lanthanum hydride](http://arxiv.org/abs/2608.10428v1)
