**Summary.** This work investigates the structural transition in superconducting lanthanum hydride ($	ext{LaH}_{10}$) by showing it is governed by a quadrupolar $	ext{T}_{2g}$ order parameter. Using advanced simulations, the authors demonstrate that this transition is weakly first-order and correlates the structural instability with the high critical temperature superconductivity. The findings provide theoretical guidance for designing new high-$T_c$ superconductors.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state physics, the use of advanced simulation techniques like $	ext{PIMD}$ for anharmonic effects and mapping phase boundaries using order parameters is relevant to understanding quantum many-body dynamics in strongly coupled systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to characterize the nature of the structural phase transition and its connection to high superconductivity in lanthanum hydride ($	ext{LaH}_{10}$).

**Main result.** The structural transition is driven by a quadrupolar $	ext{T}_{2g}$ order parameter and exhibits a weak first-order character, which is linked to the discontinuous softening of optical phonons.

**Method.** The research employs cluster multipole moment analysis (CMP) and Path Integral Molecular Dynamics ($	ext{PIMD}$) simulations coupled with advanced neural network potentials trained on DFT data.

**Model / system.** The system studied is lanthanum hydride ($	ext{LaH}_{10}$), a superhydride known for its high critical temperature ($T_c = 250 	ext{ K}$). The analysis focuses on the structural changes in the hydrogen sublattice under pressure.

**Key observables.** Quadrupolar order parameter, soft optical phonon frequencies ($	ext{T}_{2g}$ modes), total energy and volume discontinuities across the phase boundary.

**Important parameters / regimes.** High pressures (up to $\sim 170 	ext{ GPa}$) and temperatures; the transition is characterized as weakly first-order.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping structural instabilities via multipole moments and phonon softening, assuming these are key ingredients for high-$T_c$ superconductivity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the $	ext{Fm}ar{3}m 	o 	ext{R}ar{3}m$ structural change, showing soft optical phonons that soften, hit a floor, and jump discontinuously with pressure. Other figures map order parameters and phase boundaries.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the symmetry breaking using CMP moments to identify the quadrupolar order parameter, corroborates this finding by observing phonon softening/jumps via PIMD simulations, and confirms the weak first-order nature through thermodynamic calculations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Lanthanum hydride (LaH$_{10}$) has been widely studied for its high superconducting critical temperature of 250 K at about 170 GPa pressure. Although the structural ${R\bar{3}m}$-to-${Fm\bar{3}m}$ transition under pressure connected to the emergence of the superconducting phase in this material is broadly understood, the detailed characterization of its nature and its order parameter are still missing. By applying the cluster multipole moment analysis to the hydrogen sublattice, we reveal that this transition is triggered by a quadrupolar $T_{2g}$ order parameter, and we provide evidence for its weak first-order nature. By performing path integral molecular dynamics coupled to a message-passing atomic cluster expansion (MACE) neural network potential, trained on Perdew-Burke-Ernzerhof (PBE) density functional theory configurations, we show that the collapse of the order parameter at the transition is simultaneously associated with the discontinuous softening of the optical $T_{2g}$ phonons. Their symmetry lets them carry a non-negligible electron-phonon coupling in LaH$_{10}$, while the weak first-order nature of the transition makes them soft. The presence of structural instabilities with low-frequency quadrupolar distortions can be a key ingredient to enhance superconductivity in superhydrides and provides guidance for the discovery of new high-$T_c$ superconductors in hydrogen-rich compounds.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports on using Scanning NV Magnetometry (SNVM) to image both static and microwave stray fields generated by magnetic vortices in permalloy films. The technique achieves high spatial resolution ($\sim 50 	ext{ nm}$) and successfully characterizes dynamic phenomena, such as field enhancements near the vortex core, validating its potential for developing nanoscale magnonic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While primarily condensed matter magnetism, the use of NV centers to probe nanoscale magnetic dynamics is highly relevant to quantum sensing and open quantum systems characterization, providing a platform for studying spin-field interactions at unprecedented spatial resolution.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to image and characterize ferromagnetic vortex dynamics, including both static stray fields and microwave (MW) fields, at the nanoscale.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated high-resolution imaging of these dynamic magnetic fields in permalloy structures using SNVM, achieving field enhancements up to $40	imes$ near the vortex core.

**Method.** Scanning Nitrogen-Vacancy Magnetometry (SNVM) is employed, utilizing Optically Detected Magnetic Resonance (ODMR) and Rabi oscillation measurements to map both DC and AC magnetic fields.

**Model / system.** The experiments are performed on mesoscopic permalloy ($	ext{NiFe}$) thin films patterned into square or disc geometries. The analysis is heavily supported by comparison with micromagnetic simulations solving the LLG equation.

**Key observables.** Static stray fields (measured via ODMR frequency), microwave field amplitude (derived from ODMR FWHM and Rabi oscillations), and evanescent decay constants ($k$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Spatial resolution ($\sim 50 	ext{ nm}$), MW field enhancement factors ($\sim 40	imes$), and characteristic decay lengths for different vortex modes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The technique assumes the power-broadened regime relationship between FWHM and $B_{	ext{MW}}$ holds, and that micromagnetic simulations accurately capture the underlying physics despite disorder effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate ODMR/Rabi maps comparing experimental measurements to simulation predictions for both square (wall mode) and disc (azimuthal mode) vortex structures, alongside analyses of field decay with distance.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by detailing the SNVM measurement techniques (static vs. dynamic fields), presenting results on patterned permalloy samples (square/disc), comparing these findings against detailed micromagnetic simulations, and analyzing specific physical phenomena like field enhancement and wave decay.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The generation and manipulation of spin waves at the nanoscale via magnetic vortices are of considerable importance because of their broad applications across magnonic and quantum technologies. Previously, fixed nitrogen-vacancy (NV) centers in diamond have been used to locally characterize vortex dynamics, and scanning NV magnetometry (SNVM) has been used to image vortices' static stray fields. Here, we demonstrate SNVM imaging of both the static and microwave fields generated by vortices in mesoscopic permalloy structures with $\sim$50 nm spatial resolution, achieving excellent agreement with micromagnetic simulations, while revealing the effects of disorder. We further demonstrate a 40$\times$ microwave field enhancement near a vortex core and image the disorder-dependent, spatially varying, evanescent decay of these microwaves. Our ambient, tabletop technique surpasses diffraction-limited techniques' resolutions by at least 5$\times$, with far greater accessibility and throughput than synchrotron radiation-based techniques, offering new opportunities in the study and development of magnonic devices.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors demonstrate that cathodoluminescence (CL) interferometry can be used as a novel tool to measure the time characteristics and phase of optical resonances in plasmonic and dielectric nanoparticles. By analyzing the Fourier transform of angular and frequency-resolved CL signals, they extract femtosecond decay times without needing complex ultrafast pump-probe setups. This establishes CL interferometry as a versatile method for nanoscale characterization.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, non-pump-probe technique to measure ultrafast dynamics in nanoscale optical cavities, which is highly relevant for understanding energy relaxation in quantum light sources or solid-state emitters.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Direct time-resolved measurement of optical resonances in nanophotonic systems is challenging due to ultrafast relaxation processes.

**Main result.** CL interferometry successfully accesses the temporal response and phase behavior of optical resonances on femtosecond timescales for various plasmonic and dielectric structures.

**Method.** The technique uses Fourier transformation of angle- and frequency-resolved cathodoluminescence (CL) interferograms to extract time delay information related to resonance decay times.

**Model / system.** The study investigates optical excitations in dielectric and plasmonic nanostructures, including Au nanoparticles, Au nanostars, and Si nanospheres. The measurement setup involves electron beam excitation and collection of CL signals.

**Key observables.** Resonance decay times ($	au$), spectral beating signatures, phase information, and the electric far-field amplitude $\mathbf{E}(\mathbf{k}_{\|}, \omega)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Decay times in the range of 1–10 fs; electron beam energy (30 keV); angular/spectral resolution.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on an analytical framework connecting linear temporal and spectral response via Fourier transformation, and sometimes assumes Lorentzian scatterer profiles.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the analytical calculation of CL interferometry for different scatterers and show time-domain Fourier transforms revealing interference patterns related to resonance decay times. Other figures compare spectra from various sample geometries (on-particle vs. off-particle).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops an analytical framework using CL interferometry, demonstrates its application by analyzing broadband plasmonic and dielectric emitters, and confirms the results experimentally on multiple nanoparticle systems.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Cathodoluminescence (CL) spectroscopy provides access to optical excitations with nanometer spatial resolution, but direct time-resolved measurements of optical resonances remain challenging. Here, we demonstrate that CL interferometry provides access to the temporal response, phase behavior, and modal spectral structure of resonant nanoscale scatterers without requiring ultrafast pump-probe schemes. We develop an analytical framework in which Fourier transformation angle- and frequency-resolved CL interferograms yields the decay time of optical resonances governed by the linear optical response. Multimode resonators exhibit characteristic temporal CL beating signatures associated with spectral mode splitting. By exploiting transition radiation emitted from a nearby metallic surface as a broadband reference, we further demonstrate phase retrieval and cross-correlation measurements between instantaneous and resonant emission processes. Experimental measurements on Au nanoparticles, broadband plasmonic emitters, Au nanostars, and Si nanospheres supporting multipolar Mie resonances confirm the theoretical predictions, and decay times in the range 1-10 fs are derived for each system. Our results establish CL interferometry as a powerful approach for accessing spectral, spatial, and phase information within a single nanoscale measurement with fs resolution.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study predicts strong coherent coupling between antiferromagnetic magnons and spoof surface plasmons within a novel planar heterostructure. By solving coupled Maxwell and magnetization dynamics equations, the authors confirm the existence of hybrid modes exhibiting avoided crossings in the THz regime. This work presents a highly tunable platform for on-chip quantum information transfer between spin and electromagnetic excitations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work establishes a planar, chip-compatible platform for hybrid magnon-plasmon interactions, which is highly relevant for developing integrated quantum devices that couple spin excitations (magnons) with electromagnetic modes (plasmons).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates achieving strong, coherent information exchange between antiferromagnetic (AFM) magnons and spoof surface plasmons in a planar geometry.

**Main result.** The authors predict strong magnon-plasmon coupling in the terahertz regime for this planar heterostructure, confirming operation in the strong coupling regime via calculated cooperativity values exceeding unity.

**Method.** The analysis involves analytically solving coupled Maxwell and Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert equations to derive the system's dispersion relation.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a planar heterostructure comprising an AFM thin film, a dielectric spacer, and a structured metal surface supporting spoof surface plasmons (SSPs).

**Key observables.** Pronounced avoided crossings in the dispersion relation, coupling strength ($g$), and cooperativity parameter ($C$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system is tunable via geometric parameters such as groove width-to-period ratio ($a/p$), dielectric thickness ($h$), and groove depth ($d$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Approximations include assuming small-amplitude AFM oscillations and using an effective-medium approach for the corrugated metal.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic of the planar structure, bare plasmon dispersions, and the resulting hybrid excitation dispersion curves showing avoided crossings.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by setting up the coupled electromagnetic and magnetization dynamics equations, deriving the determinant equation for the dispersion relation, and finally analyzing the coupling strength and cooperativity parameter to confirm strong coupling.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hybrid magnonic system provides a versatile platform for coherent information exchange between spin excitations and other physical degrees of freedom. While strong coupling between magnons and spoof plasmons has been observed based on ferrimagnetic spheres and localized microwave resonators, it remains unexplored whether coherent magnon-plasmon coupling can be achieved in planar magnetic structures. Here we study the hybridization of antiferromagnetic (AFM) magnons and spoof surface plasmons in a planar heterostructure consisting of an AFM thin film, a dielectric spacer, and a structured metal surface. By analytically solving the coupled Maxwell and magnetization dynamics equation, we predict strong magnon-plasmon coupling in the terahertz regime, manifested by pronounced avoided crossings in the dispersion. The coupling originates from the spatial overlap between magnonic and plasmonic modes in the dielectric spacer, and can be efficiently tuned through geometric parameters of the system. The calculated cooperativity confirms that the hybrid system can operate in the strong coupling regime, enabling coherent information transfer between magnons and plasmons. Our results establish a planar platform for plasmon-magnon hybridization, which is more amenable to on-chip manipulation and integration.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper demonstrates that applying linearly polarized light to a 2D d-wave altermagnet allows precise control over its topological state via Floquet engineering. By breaking specific symmetries, the drive induces intermediate Chern insulating phases with switchable Chern numbers ($C=\pm 1$). This tunability is experimentally verifiable through measurable changes in the optical Hall response and magneto-optical rotations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges concepts from topological band theory (Chern numbers) with time-periodic driving (Floquet engineering), providing a concrete platform for controlling electronic properties using light polarization, which is highly relevant to quantum optics and condensed matter physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how driving a 2D d-wave altermagnet with linearly polarized light can control its topological phases and magneto-optical response.

**Main result.** Linearly polarized light acts as a symmetry-selective handle, enabling tunable spin-resolved band inversion, switching between Chern insulating phases ($C=\pm 1$), and providing contact-free optical detection signatures.

**Method.** The analysis employs the high-frequency Floquet expansion on a tight-binding model, calculating linear response properties using the Kubo formalism to determine conductivities and magneto-optical rotations.

**Model / system.** The system is a two-dimensional (2D) d-wave altermagnet modeled by a four-band tight-binding Hamiltonian. The driving mechanism involves periodic excitation via linearly polarized light, which breaks the $C_{4z}T$ symmetry.

**Key observables.** Longitudinal and Hall conductivities ($\sigma_{xx}, \sigma_{xy}$), Faraday rotation ($	heta_F$), Kerr rotation ($	heta_K$), and associated ellipticities ($\eta_F, \eta_K$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Drive amplitude ($A_0$) and polarization angle ($	heta$); the system exhibits distinct phases based on these parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on an off-resonant, high-frequency Floquet expansion, assuming $\hbar\Omega_p$ exceeds the gap and bandwidth for validity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of optical conductivities ($	ext{Re}/	ext{Im}\sigma_{xy}$) and magneto-optical responses ($	heta_F, 	heta_K$) on both the driving amplitude ($A_0$) and polarization angle ($	heta$), showing characteristic sign reversals.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the static topological phase (spin-Chern insulator) and then introduces Floquet driving. It details how the drive breaks symmetry via anisotropic renormalization, leading to tunable Chern phases. Finally, it calculates and analyzes the resulting frequency-dependent optical conductivities and magneto-optical responses.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study how Floquet driving with linearly polarized light controls the topology and magneto-optical response of a two-dimensional (2D) $d$-wave altermagnet. In the absence of linearly polarized optical field and under spin conservation, we find that the system hosts a spin-Chern (a quantum-spin-Hall analog) phase with Chern numbers of opposite sign in the two spin sectors. The irradiated optical field breaks the $C_{4z}\mathcal{T}$ crystalline antiunitary symmetry between the spin sectors. Symmetry breaking originates from polarization-dependent Peierls phases, which renormalize hopping anisotropically along the two axes. The resulting spin-selective gap closures produce intermediate Chern-insulating phases with $C=\pm1$. The drive amplitude $A_0$ determines the inversion thresholds, while rotating the polarization by $π/2$ swaps the spin sectors and reverses the Chern number. Using the Kubo formalism, we compute the frequency-dependent longitudinal and Hall conductivities and derive the corresponding Faraday and Kerr rotations for a free-standing conducting sheet. The longitudinal response tracks the Floquet-renormalized interband thresholds, whereas the optical Hall response, together with the sign of the magneto-optical rotations, distinguishes the two opposite Berry-curvature chiralities. Sizable Kerr angles occur only within narrow resonant windows and should be interpreted together with the reflected intensity and Kerr ellipticity. These results identify linearly polarized light as a symmetry-selective handle for spin-resolved band inversion, Chern-number switching, and contact-free optical detection in $d$-wave altermagnets.

</details>

**Summary.** This pedagogical paper demonstrates how Angle-Resolved Photoemission Spectroscopy (ARPES) serves as an experimental tool to visualize the Fermi gas model. By measuring electron energy versus momentum, researchers can map out the band structure of metals, confirming predictions derived from quantum mechanics regarding the filling of electronic states up to the Fermi level.

**Why it may be interesting.** The paper bridges fundamental quantum statistical mechanics (Fermi gas) with advanced experimental techniques (ARPES), providing a tangible link between abstract theoretical models and measurable electronic properties in real materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To use experimental measurements from Angle-Resolved Photoemission Spectroscopy (ARPES) to visualize and understand the fundamental concepts of the Fermi gas model in metals.

**Main result.** ARPES successfully maps the energy-momentum dispersion relation $E(\vec{k})$, allowing direct visualization of theoretical predictions like the parabolic band structure and the resulting circular Fermi surface.

**Method.** The analysis combines quantum mechanical principles (photoelectric effect, momentum/energy conservation) with solid-state physics models (particle-in-a-box, free electron gas) to interpret experimental ARPES data.

**Model / system.** The system is electrons in crystalline solids, initially modeled by the Fermi gas concept derived from quantum mechanics. The analysis uses concepts like periodic boundary conditions and the Pauli exclusion principle to define the filled states up to the Fermi energy ($E_F$).

**Key observables.** Energy vs. Momentum dispersion relation $E(\vec{k})$, Fermi Energy ($E_F$), Fermi Surface radius ($k_F$), Group Velocity ($v_g$), and Effective Mass ($m^*$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Electron density ($N/V$), Temperature ($T$), UV photon energy, and the chemical potential ($\mu \approx E_F$ at low T).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model initially assumes minimal inter-electron interactions (free electron gas) and relies on momentum conservation during photoemission. The analysis shifts from simple box models to periodic boundary conditions for crystalline solids.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between theoretical free electron gas predictions, 3D k-space spheres defining the Fermi surface, and actual ARPES spectra showing constant energy contours at $E_F$.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing basic quantum principles (photoelectric effect) to deriving the continuum limit of the particle-in-a-box model for the Fermi gas. It then details how ARPES measures this $E(\vec{k})$ relation, allowing characterization of band structure features like the Fermi surface and effective mass.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES) can measure electrons' energy vs momentum relations in solids via photoelectric effect. This pedagogical paper experimental results from this technique as a tool for visualizing the Fermi gas model in quantum statistical mechanics. This model is an important starting point for understanding more complex electronic behaviors in real metals.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the spin-wave excitation spectrum in ferromagnetic vortices stabilized by DMI. The authors demonstrate that the DMI causes a large, measurable splitting of azimuthal modes ($\Delta\omega$), an effect that remains robust even when vortex core contributions are removed. This deepens understanding of chiral magnetic textures and their dynamic response.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on magnetism, the study of collective excitations (spin waves) in confined magnetic textures provides a model system for understanding wave dynamics and topological effects in condensed matter systems relevant to quantum information storage or spintronics devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To investigate the giant mode splitting of azimuthal spin waves in radial vortices, attributing this effect primarily to the Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction (DMI) even when vortex core effects are absent.

**Main result.** The DMI induces a large and robust frequency splitting ($\Delta\omega$) in the spin wave spectrum that increases significantly with both the DMI constant ($D$) and the azimuthal mode index ($|m|$).

**Method.** Theoretical analysis using continuum models, deriving equations of motion from the LLG equation, and confirming results via micromagnetic simulations.

**Model / system.** The system is a ferromagnetic nanodisk or ring hosting magnetic vortices stabilized by interfacial DMI. The energy includes exchange, DMI, and dipolar interactions, governing spin-wave dynamics.

**Key observables.** Mode splitting ($\Delta\omega$) between $\pm m$ azimuthal modes; vortex core radius ($r_{VC}$); frequency spectra ($\omega$ vs. $D$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** DMI constant ($D$), mode index ($|m|$), and the geometry (disk vs. ring with a central hole).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Simplification of dipolar energy by retaining only surface charge contributions; linearization of the LLG equation for spin-wave analysis.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate vortex transitions based on DMI strength, show $\Delta\omega$ dependence on $D$, and demonstrate that mode splitting persists in rings (where VC effects are eliminated) due to DMI.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical model and Hamiltonian to deriving theoretical expressions for mode splitting. It then uses micromagnetic simulations to confirm that DMI is the dominant source of splitting, especially when comparing full disks to ring geometries.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Radial vortex is a topological spin texture stabilized by the interfacial Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction (DMI) in ferromagnetic disks. Previous investigations have shown that the doublet splitting of azimuthal modes in traditional circular vortices arises from the coupling between azimuthal spin waves and vortex core (VC), an effect occurs only for azimuthal indices $m=\pm1$ and is absent for higher-order modes. Here, we present a giant mode splitting of azimuthal spin waves in radial vortices, even in the absence of the VC. This mode splitting arises from the DMI, which can be an order of magnitude larger than that induced by the VC. Moreover, the DMI-induced frequency splitting increases with both the DMI constant and mode index, reaching tens of GHz for higher-order azimuthal modes. Our results reveal a robust mechanism for mode splitting in chiral magnetic textures and deepen the fundamental understanding of the DMI effect on the spin-wave dynamics in confined magnets.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study uses DFT calculations to map out the physical properties of the high-pressure electride superconductor Li5N. By analyzing structural stability, electronic structure, and mechanical response across 150–350 GPa, the authors predict that Li5N is a stable material with tunable multifunctional characteristics under extreme pressure.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state physics rather than quantum information, the study's rigorous application of first-principles methods to predict emergent properties (superconductivity, electride behavior) under extreme conditions is highly relevant for understanding strongly correlated or novel quantum materials systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to theoretically investigate the physical properties of the electride superconductor Li5N under high pressure for potential multifunctional applications.

**Main result.** Li5N is predicted to be chemically and dynamically stable within the 150–350 GPa range, exhibiting metallic behavior and suggesting potential for tuning its properties through pressure.

**Method.** The research employs first-principles calculations using Density Functional Theory (DFT) with the PBEsol functional, supplemented by DFPT for phonon and optical response analysis.

**Model / system.** The physical system is Li5N, an electride solid, whose properties are calculated as a function of applied pressure in the range of 150–350 GPa. The calculations model structural changes, electronic band structure, and mechanical responses under extreme conditions.

**Key observables.** Lattice parameters (a, c), density ($ho$), formation energy ($E_f$), elastic constants ($C_{ij}$), phonon dispersion curves, electronic DOS at the Fermi level, and optical dielectric function components ($\epsilon_1, \epsilon_2$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the variation of lattice parameters, density, and total energy with pressure. Stability is confirmed by positive phonon frequencies across the studied pressure range.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a standard theoretical materials science structure: introduction defining the problem, detailed methodology (DFT/DFPT), presentation of structural stability results under varying pressure, electronic analysis (DOS, band structure), mechanical characterization (elastic constants), and finally, optical response analysis.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This study aims to unveil the physical properties of multifunctional Li5N electride under high pressure in the range of 150-350 GPa through first principles analysis within the density functional theory.

**Summary.** This work addresses the predictive gap in characterizing HOTIs formed by cleaving an OAI. By analyzing corner states, the authors demonstrate that fractional charges emerge when cleavage creates a balanced charge compensation involving interstice vacancies. The findings establish a strict geometric and electronic requirement—the orientation of dangling bonds—that dictates the existence of these higher-order topological features.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit link between geometric cleavage, charge compensation (fractional charge vs. vacancy), and the required directionality of electronic structure (dangling bonds) provides a powerful, physically grounded diagnostic tool for predicting topological phases in complex materials.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The predictive power of topological quantum chemistry based on local charge profiles is insufficient for determining the electronic structure upon crystalline cleavage in Higher-Order Topological Insulators (HOTIs).

**Main result.** Cleaving an obstructed atomic insulator reveals a topological phase characterized by e/2 fractional charges at specific corners, balanced by complementary vacancies of interstice charge.

**Method.** The study employs tight-binding models and analyzes real-space properties using Wannier functions to map out the emergence of zero-energy corner modes dictated by dangling bond orientation.

**Model / system.** The research investigates HOTI phases within an Obstructed Atomic Insulator (OAI) modeled by a three-term hopping Hamiltonian. The analysis focuses on superlattice patterns and the resulting boundary conditions upon cleavage.

**Key observables.** Topological zero-energy double-degenerate e/2 fractional charges, complementary interstice charge vacancies, anisotropic evolution of the Dirac mass term, and lower entanglement entropy at topological corners compared to the bulk.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system is analyzed across various superlattice patterns (1-8) controlled by parameters like magnetic flux and atomic arrangement.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the emergence of zero modes is strictly dictated by the exposure and directionality of dangling bonds acting as the mass term for a Dirac fermion.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate crystal cleavage evolution across 8 patterns, showing where zero-energy states appear; they also compare real-space charge distributions (atom vs. bond centers) and map energy/entanglement entropy differences between topological corners and the bulk.

**Paper structure.** The paper first identifies the limitations of current TQC methods for HOTI cleavage. It then models the system using a tight-binding Hamiltonian, analyzing various superlattice patterns to locate topologically non-trivial phases (patterns 1, 4, 5, 8). The core argument develops by linking fractional charges and vacancies to the massive Dirac fermion criterion, concluding with entropy/energy mapping.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Topological quantum chemistry based on local charge profiles lacks predictive power for the crystalline cleavage of higher-order topological insulators (HOTIs). By cleaving an obstructed atomic insulator, we discover a topological phase characterized by e/2 fractional charges localized at precisely half of the corners, while the remaining empty corners host complementary vacancies of interstice charge. These zero-energy charge-vacancies and topological corners form a spatially balanced geometry, confined separately by C2 rotation symmetry. Crucially, we demonstrate that the emergence of corner zero modes dictates that specific dangling bonds-acting as the mass of a Dirac fermion-must explicitly expose in, and subtly slope toward, the corner regions. This strict directionality is verified by the anisotropic evolution of the mass term within a (2+1)-dimensional parameter space. Moreover, we find that the topological corners acquire lower entanglement entropy compared to the bulk, a behavior opposite to that of the real-space energy distribution.

</details>
